arXiv:2610.20442v1  [cs.RO]  14 Oct 2026
Towards Agentic Robots:
Model, Data, Environment, and Harness
Chonghan Qin, Dongming Wu, Kaituo Feng, Jiaxu Wang, Xinlei Yu, Peiwen Sun
Yijia Fan, Xilin He, Bingao Chen, Weitong Lian, Ryan Ji, Wenqi Ren, Xiaobin Hu
Dahua Lin†, Yu-Gang Jiang†, Xiangyu Yue†
MMLab, The Chinese University of Hong Kong
Fudan University
NovaXBot
Tsinghua University
Zhejiang University
Sun Yat-sen University
Abstract
Foundation models are driving a paradigm shift in robotic intelligence, moving beyond low-level
action prediction to goal-directed orchestration of data collection, model training, task planning,
and real-world execution. We characterize this emerging paradigm as an agentic robots, in which
agents reason over human instructions, interact with robotic environments, rectify execution
errors autonomously, and dynamically adjust operational strategies. Although this field develops
fast, current studies are not well connected. Researchers have no consistent view of how to
combine different agentic functions into a complete robot system.
This paper provides a unified overview of existing research on agentic robots. We first trace the
development from robot policies to agentic systems, covering vision-language-action models,
world models, and world-action models. We then clarify the definition and scope of agentic
robots and organize existing research into seventeen categories across four functional domains:
Model, Data, Environment, and Harness. Across these domains, we examine how agents
shape model learning and robot decisions, collect and curate robot data, construct worlds, tasks,
and tests for learning and evaluation, and organize capabilities for robot use. Finally, we discuss
open challenges and outline directions for future research. Looking forward, we hope this work
contributes to a clearer understanding of agentic robots and supports new approaches to making
them more capable, reliable, and adaptive.

1 Introduction
Over the past few years, foundation models including large language models (LLMs) and multimodal
large language models (MLLMs) have greatly advanced robotic policy learning. They endow robots with
novel capabilities that traditional task-specific models lack, especially in scene and task understanding and
cross-scene generalization, by scaling robot datasets and model size [20, 256]. As a dominant technical
solution in this field, vision-language-action (VLA) models utilize either discrete action tokens or diffusion-
or flow-matching-based continuous action prediction strategies to connect robotic action prediction and
the semantic knowledge embedded in pre-trained LLMs [13, 19, 269]. Nevertheless, improved action
prediction alone cannot guarantee reliable execution for long-horizon tasks. These tasks demand robots to
track task progress, adjust plans amid environmental changes, and recover from failures. VLA policies by
themselves cannot consistently deliver these capabilities.
Why Agentic Robots Matter? Recently, agentic robots are attracting surging attention in the research
community, which represent a new generation of embodied intelligent systems that integrate foundation
model reasoning with embodied agency and environmental interaction capabilities. General-purpose
models such as GPT-6 Astra [258] and Gemini 4 Argon [87] are opening new possibilities for such systems.
For example, GPT-6 Astra demonstrates promising capabilities as a robot manipulation agent, using
visual observations to generate end-effector commands and adjust its actions from execution feedback
without task-specific fine-tuning [439]. On RoboDojo, it achieves a 22.48% average success rate across
42 simulation tasks, outperforming the trained robot policy DM0.5 (19.34%) [59], GalaxeaVLA G0.5
(14.88%) [76], Xiaomi-Robotics-1 (13.93%) [384], OpenWAM-𝛼(11.92%) [363] and 𝜋0.5 (6.91%) [269].
Despite this progress, inference latency and resource costs highlight the importance of how foundation
models are integrated into robotic systems. Beyond optimizing and scaling model parameters, agentic
robots use foundation models to plan actions, monitor task progress, and revise decisions based on
execution feedback. Their capabilities therefore depend on both the model and the tools, observations,
and feedback available in the environment. The agentic robot paradigm addresses the “brain in a vat”
limitation of previous robotic solutions, shifting robotic intelligence from static pattern matching toward
adaptive autonomous operation.
Agentic Robots Need a Taxonomy. Despite growing interest, research on agentic robots remains
fragmented across model learning, data acquisition, environment construction, and runtime system design.
3


--- PAGE 4 ---
These directions are often studied separately, although their contributions interact within a robotic system.
Related terms such as embodied agents, autonomous robots, and self-improving robotic systems further
blur the scope of the field by emphasizing different capabilities without consistently clarifying their
boundaries. Together, these issues make it difficult to identify where agents make decisions and how those
decisions contribute to the overall system. A unified taxonomy should therefore distinguish the roles of
individual components while explaining how they work together. To this end, we organize agentic robotics
into four functional domains: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness, tracing the agent’s decisions
within each domain and their effects across the system. Figure 2 shows how these four domains work
together in agentic robot systems.
Key Questions
1
Model: What should the agent learn, understand, decide, and control?
2
Data: How should the agent collect, annotate, and refine robot data?
3
Environment: What worlds, tasks, and tests should the agent build?
4
Harness: How should robot capabilities be orchestrated, recovered, and evolved?
The four questions above guide our discussion of how agents contribute to robotic systems. Section 4
examines how agents support model learning and robot decision-making, while Section 5 explains how
they collect, annotate, and refine robot data. Section 6 considers the worlds, tasks, and tests agents
construct for learning and evaluation. Section 7 examines how agents coordinate executable capabilities,
monitor execution, recover from failures, and retain useful knowledge for subsequent tasks.
Contributions. This paper makes three main contributions:
• A Unified Illustration for Agentic Robotics. We trace the evolution from robot policies to agentic
systems and clarify the role of agents in robot task execution and capability development. We
introduce a taxonomy spanning Model, Data, Environment, and Harness, bringing these research
directions into a common framework.
• A Systematic Analysis of Agentic Methods. We synthesize the mechanisms through which agents
acquire, coordinate, and improve robotic capabilities. By comparing representative approaches
across the taxonomy, we explain how agent decisions depend on available tools, robot executors,
and interaction feedback, and examine the strengths and limitations of these approaches.
• A Research Agenda for Practical Agentic Robots. Drawing on this analysis, we outline research
directions for establishing agents’ contributions and translating current advances into reliable,
efficient robot operation. The agenda connects fair evaluation with the challenges of physical
deployment and generalization across robotic platforms.
2 Evolution of Robot Learning
Robot learning spans four connected stages: learning executable behavior, integrating multimodal
knowledge, predicting physical outcomes, and coordinating execution and improvement. (1) Conventional
Robot Policies: Learning Executable Behavior. Reinforcement and imitation learning map observations
to actions, while generalist policies share experience across tasks and embodiments. Their capabilities
depend largely on the robot experience available during training. (2) Vision-Language-Action Models:
Connecting Knowledge to Action. VLAs bring the visual and linguistic knowledge of pretrained
foundation models into control, connecting the interpretation of scenes, instructions, and semantic
relationships to action generation. (3) World Models and World-Action Models: Anticipating Physical
Outcomes. These models connect action selection and generation to predictions of environmental change,
using representations of future states, observations, and their relation to actions. (4) Agentic Robots:
4


--- PAGE 5 ---
Organizing Execution and Improvement. Agentic systems extend model-mediated decisions to selecting
and coordinating capabilities, acquiring experience, and using feedback to guide subsequent execution
and capability improvement. These stages describe overlapping research traditions and complementary
capabilities that can coexist in one system.
2.1 Conventional Robot Policies
Conventional robot policies learn from demonstrations or reward-based experience. We group visual
policies by scene representation: image-based policies use image features, while 3D-aware policies
introduce geometric structure for action prediction. This distinction concerns scene representation, not
action dimensionality.
Image-based Policies. Early policies learn actions from camera images. Guided policy search [168] maps
images and robot state to motor torques, while QT-Opt [143] learns an image-conditioned action-value
function for grasping. RoboMimic [238] studies visual behavior cloning from offline demonstrations.
Visual pretraining supplies reusable policy features: R3M [244] pretrains an image encoder on human
videos for downstream imitation learning. Beyond visual encoding, ACT [449] and Diffusion Policy [55]
model action sequences with a conditional variational autoencoder and conditional diffusion, respectively.
Task conditioning extends these policies to multiple behaviors: BC-Z [128] and RT-1 [20] use task
descriptions, while VIMA [138] combines multimodal prompts with object-centered image tokens.
Cross-robot datasets broaden training: RT-1-X [260] draws on cross-robot data from Open X-Embodiment,
and Octo [256] combines heterogeneous image observations with a transformer and a diffusion action
head.
3D-aware Policies. These policies use scene geometry to predict actions. Transporter Networks [427]
construct orthographic color-height maps from RGB-D observations to predict spatial pick-and-place
actions, using a 2.5D scene representation. PerAct [298] represents observations and action locations
with 3D voxels, while RVT [88] renders virtual views of a reconstructed point cloud and combines their
predictions in 3D. Act3D [83] lifts image features into a 3D feature field and samples action locations
from coarse to fine. These geometric representations also support generative action models: DP3 [426]
conditions action diffusion on compact features encoded from sparse point clouds, whereas 3D Diffuser
Actor [146] uses spatially located scene tokens and 3D attention to denoise end-effector trajectories.
Mamba Policy [25] retains point-cloud conditioning and combines selective state-space models with
attention in its denoising network. Other methods learn geometric representations for downstream
policies. GSRL [341] pretrains and freezes a generalizable Gaussian predictor, then supplies its predicted
3D Gaussian scene representation to offline reinforcement-learning algorithms. Structural latent point
pretraining [137] regularizes point coordinates and features with a point-wise latent variational autoencoder
and uses Gaussian rendering supervision; its latent representation is then supplied to an ACT policy. Scene
representation, learning objective, and action decoder remain distinct, combinable design choices.
2.2 Vision-Language-Action Models
General VLA Models. VLAs adapt large-scale pretrained vision–language backbones to generate robot
actions, as in RT-2 [19], OpenVLA [149], and RoboFlamingo [182]. Earlier policies already used
language conditioning and pretrained encoders; VLAs connect broader knowledge of objects, relations,
and instructions to action prediction through training on robot trajectories. 𝜏0-VLA [24] combines a
high-level subtask policy with a flow-matching action expert, using execution memory and selective
world-model-guided test-time search for subtask selection.
Action Representation. A central choice is how to decode robot actions. RT-2 [19] and OpenVLA [149]
discretize continuous actions into tokens for sequence generation. Diffusion- and flow-based approaches,
including RDT-1B [217], 𝜋0 [13], 𝜋0.5 [269], CogACT [177], TinyVLA [371], GR00T N1 [254],
SmolVLA [299], DexVLA [372], InternVLA-M1 [43], LingBot-VLA [378], and Qwen-VLA [349],
5


--- PAGE 6 ---
condition continuous action decoders on visual and language features to generate action sequences.
FAST [265] compresses action sequences into compact tokens, while OpenVLA-OFT [150] combines
parallel decoding and action chunking to predict multiple future actions in one forward pass. Vela [187]
represents future motion with cubic B-splines, using a flow-matching expert to jointly predict control
points and a motion-dependent temporal span. StarVLA [305] provides a modular open-source framework
for training and evaluating combinations of vision–language backbones and action decoders.
VLA with Memory. History and memory provide temporal context when a single observation does
not reveal earlier actions or task progress. RoboFlamingo [182] aggregates successive vision–language
features with an LSTM policy head. MemoryVLA [295] retrieves perceptual and cognitive features
from a memory bank and fuses them with current representations to condition a diffusion action expert.
MEM [324] combines a video encoder for short-term observation history in its low-level policy with
language summaries of past events in its high-level policy. NativeMEM [365] reuses the pretrained VLA’s
vision encoder to compress each historical frame from each camera view into a single memory token,
allowing the policy to attend to long-term visual history alongside current observations.
VLA with Reasoning. Intermediate reasoning and semantic prediction provide explicit guidance between
task instructions and robot actions. RT-H [10] and 𝜋0.5 [269] introduce language descriptions of motions
or semantic subtasks that condition subsequent actions. ECoT [425] further combines task reasoning
with spatial information, including object locations and end-effector positions, to guide action prediction.
OneTwoVLA [203] and Gemini Robotics 1.5 [320] integrate reasoning with action generation, allowing
task interpretation to inform the robot’s next behavior.
Visual subgoals provide a complementary
representation: CoT-VLA [447] predicts an intermediate goal image and conditions subsequent actions
on the desired scene configuration. Together, these approaches provide explicit intermediate guidance at
different levels, from task decomposition and motion descriptions to spatial reasoning and visual goals,
connecting the robot’s understanding of the task with the actions required to execute it.
VLA with Imagination. Future prediction connects VLA action learning to how scenes evolve during
robot interaction. GR-1 [375], GR-2 [32], UP-VLA [434], WorldVLA [29], and RynnVLA-002 [28]
combine action learning with future-image prediction, using visual changes as additional supervision
for robot control. FLARE [456], VLA-JEPA [309], and World Guidance [307] instead model future
observations in latent space, learning predictive features or state transitions without reconstructing images.
These objectives incorporate future information into policy representations, although future images
need not be generated during execution. BagelVLA [111] further interleaves language reasoning, visual
prediction, and action generation, combining semantic task guidance with visual anticipation within the
action execution loop.
2.3 World Models and World-Action Models
World models learn how the environment changes in response to robot actions.
Predicting future
states or observations supports planning over candidate actions and policy learning from imagined
interactions [73, 96, 98, 377]. DINO-WM [459] and V-JEPA 2-AC [5] predict action-conditioned visual
features and optimize action sequences toward visual goals through model-predictive control.
World Model as Environment. Action-conditioned video models simulate interactions for policy learning
and evaluation. UniSim [400] simulates visual outcomes of instructions and low-level controls to train
embodied planners and policies. Cosmos [251] provides pretrained diffusion and autoregressive video
models for adaptation to robot-specific prediction. Cosmos-Predict2.5 [252] unifies text-, image-, and
video-conditioned generation and supports action-conditioned robot rollouts after adaptation. Genie
Envisioner [201] and DreamDojo [78] use action-conditioned video prediction for policy evaluation; the
latter also learns interaction dynamics from human videos with inferred latent actions before adapting
to target robots. Cosmos 3 [253] extends the series toward shared multimodal understanding, world
generation, and robot action modeling.
6


--- PAGE 7 ---
4D World Model. 4D world modeling captures how 3D scene geometry evolves over time, supporting
geometric planning and interaction simulation. PointWorld [121] represents robot actions as 3D point flows
and predicts the resulting scene-point displacements over a temporal horizon, supporting sampling-based
trajectory optimization. TesserAct [454] jointly generates RGB, depth, and surface-normal videos,
reconstructs temporally consistent 3D point clouds, and uses a point-cloud inverse dynamics model to infer
actions. 4DGen [224] uses cross-view pointmap supervision to generate geometrically consistent multi-
view RGB-D futures, from which a pose tracker recovers end-effector trajectories. MVISTA-4D [342]
generates synchronized, view-consistent RGB-D futures from a single-view observation. It optimizes a
trajectory latent at test time to match the imagined future and refines the decoded actions with residual
inverse dynamics. Kinema4D [394] converts robot commands into kinematically computed 4D trajectories
and uses their projected pointmaps to condition joint RGB and pointmap generation, simulating the
surrounding environment’s response.
World Model as Planner. World models supply visual plans.
Inverse dynamics maps predicted
observations or latents to robot actions. UniPi [62] and RoboDreamer [463] use inverse dynamics to
translate language-conditioned visual plans into actions. Seer [323] couples visual foresight and inverse
dynamics in an end-to-end model, conditioning action prediction on latent representations of the forecasted
visual state. GE-Act 2.0 [2] predicts future visual latents in a single generator pass, then combines
them with current visual latents and proprioception in an inverse dynamics model to produce an action
chunk. This connection also extends to geometric plans: TesserAct [454] infers actions from reconstructed
point-cloud sequences, while MVISTA-4D [342] combines test-time trajectory-latent optimization with
residual inverse dynamics to refine actions toward the imagined future. These approaches depend on both
the feasibility of the predicted future and the inverse ability to translate it into robot-specific control.
Joint Video–Action Modeling. World-action models (WAMs) combine future visual prediction with
robot action learning, linking behavior to its environmental consequences. UVA [179], UWM [468],
and Motus [12] jointly learn video prediction and action generation within a shared framework. 𝜏0-
WM [462] unifies video–action generation and action-conditioned simulation. At test time, predicted task
progress scores candidate futures; when initial candidates are unreliable, a selected future guides action
refinement. DreamZero [408], LingBot-VA [176], Cosmos Policy [151], GigaWorld-Policy [406], and
Being-H0.7 [230] connect action learning to future visual information through observation prediction or
latent alignment. Fast-WAM [422] learns efficient action generation through video co-training without
future imagination at inference. ImageWAM [442] guides action generation with pretrained image-editing
features without decoding future images. MaskWAM [418] combines optional initial mask prompts with
joint future mask, RGB, and action prediction to focus control on task-relevant regions. A recent direction
jointly models language, video, and action: UniWAM [303] connects a physical reasoner, world generator,
and action predictor through joint multimodal attention, learning language outputs alongside future visual
predictions and actions.
2.4 Agentic Robots
Agentic robots organize robotic execution and capability improvement. Given a goal, they coordinate
capabilities, interpret execution feedback, and adjust subsequent operations [119, 125, 196, 297]. Learned
policies, including VLAs and world-action models, supply executable capabilities within this process,
shifting attention to how behavior is selected, coordinated, and revised during execution.
This perspective also extends to data collection, model training, and experimental deployment. Agentic
decisions organize experience acquisition, guide learning experiments, and use evaluation feedback to
improve capabilities [233, 383]. These decisions connect current execution with improvements for future
tasks. Following section examine four functional domains, Model, Data, Environment, and Harness,
that describe where these decisions act and how robotic capabilities are acquired, used, and improved.

Figure 3. Taxonomy and literature landscape of agentic robotic systems. Four functional domains cover seventeen
non-exclusive categories: Model covers learning and robot-facing decisions. Data covers the acquisition and curation
of data. Environment covers worlds, tasks, and evaluation. Harness covers how robotic capabilities are coordinated,
reused, and improved to support agentic task execution.
3 Foundations of Agentic Robots
The approaches discussed in Section 2 provide capabilities for perception, reasoning, action prediction, and
world modeling. Building agentic robots requires coordinating these capabilities through task planning,
tool use, and execution feedback, while also supporting the development of new robot capabilities. To
characterize these systems, this section first defines agentic robots and their scope, then introduces a
taxonomy spanning Model, Data, Environment, and Harness, and finally compares agentic robotics with
related concepts.
3.1 Definition and Scope
We define agentic robots as robotic systems in which agents autonomously plan and coordinate task
execution using environmental observations and execution feedback. Given a goal, these agents determine
the steps needed to achieve it, select and coordinate actions, skills, or tools, and monitor progress without
requiring a human to specify every step. As execution proceeds, they use new observations and outcomes
to decide whether to continue, gather more information, revise the plan, or recover from failure. We
consider such systems on both physical robots and in simulation.
These capabilities can be implemented within an integrated model or through a combination of models,
policies, programs, and tools. For example, when retrieving an object from a drawer, the agent may
select an opening skill, check whether the drawer has opened, and decide whether to retrieve the object
or revise the opening attempt. The defining consideration is how the agent organizes and adjusts task
execution to achieve the goal. Action prediction or low-level feedback control alone does not establish
these capabilities.
Beyond task execution, agents can also contribute to developing robot capabilities. They may collect and
refine robot data, construct tasks and environments, revise training procedures, or improve robot skills
and execution software. Feedback from interaction and experiments can guide these changes and help
improve performance on subsequent tasks. Our paper examines these contributions across Model, Data,
Environment, and Harness, covering how agents support robot operation and capability development.
8


--- PAGE 9 ---
Comparison with Current Works. PAPAV [47] compares computer use and robot use through Perceive,
Anticipate, Plan, Act, and Verify, emphasizing the consequences of physical embodiment. [287] examines
model–robot integration through protocol interfaces, orchestration, and embedded policies. [129] surveys
robot-learning approaches spanning action-predicting weights and executable skills. [250] studies the
executable software that organizes agent execution. [331] examines where verification signals come from
and whether they are available and trustworthy. Our focus is how these components work together during
robot use and capability building.
3.2 Taxonomy
We organize existing work into four domains according to the roles agents play in developing and operating
robotic systems: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness. Model concerns how agents use and improve
models for robotic perception, planning, control, and adaptation. Data concerns how agents collect,
annotate, and refine data for robot learning. Environment concerns how agents construct scenes, generate
tasks, and design evaluations for robot training and assessment. Harness concerns how agents coordinate
models, tools, and robot skills to execute tasks, using memory and execution feedback to monitor progress
and recover from failures. Together, these domains describe the roles of agents across robot capability
development and task execution. Figure 3 presents the overall taxonomy, and Table 1 defines the seventeen
categories within these domains.
The taxonomy distinguishes functional contributions rather than assigning each robotic system to a single
category. These contributions may span domains: Zero2Skill [334], for example, links agent-directed data
collection and preparation to subsequent robot policy training. They may also operate together within
a domain, as in HarnessVLA [441], which integrates memory, orchestration, monitoring, and recovery
around fixed executors. ENPIRE [383] illustrates how a research agent coordinates experiments to improve
robot policies, connecting experiment management with capability development. We therefore discuss
each work under the categories corresponding to its relevant functions, allowing a paper to appear in
multiple categories when it makes contributions to more than one.
3.3 Comparison with Related Concepts
Agentic robotics is closely related to embodied AI, autonomous robotics, and robot foundation models.
These concepts overlap in their methods and applications, but emphasize different aspects of robotic
intelligence. We clarify their relationships using the scope adopted in this paper.
Agentic Robotics vs. Embodied AI. Embodied AI studies intelligence through an agent’s interaction
with its environment, encompassing tasks such as exploration, navigation, manipulation, and embodied
question answering. Both embodied AI and agentic robotics connect perception, reasoning, and action in
physical or simulated environments [63, 396]. The distinction concerns the emphasis placed on how these
capabilities are organized. Embodied AI broadly investigates learning and acting through environmental
interaction; our agentic perspective focuses on how agents select capabilities, coordinate task execution,
and revise decisions using feedback. This focus is consistent with work examining foundation models as
agents that invoke and coordinate robot functionality [287]. It also extends to capability development:
AutoRT, for example, uses foundation models to propose and filter tasks for robot data collection [3].
Agentic Robotics vs. Autonomous Robotics. Autonomous robotics concerns robots that carry out tasks
without continuous human control. Its established research already integrates planning, acting, monitoring,
observation, and learning to support deliberate behavior in changing environments. These functions
therefore constitute shared foundations, rather than capabilities unique to agentic robotics [126]. Within
this broader field, our paper examines how foundation model agents participate in these functions by
interpreting goals, coordinating skills or tools, and incorporating execution feedback into subsequent
decisions. Inner Monologue illustrates this connection by returning observations and skill outcomes
to a language model for continued planning [119]. Accordingly, “agentic” identifies how decisions are
9


--- PAGE 10 ---
organized within the system; it does not imply complete independence from human guidance or a higher
level of autonomy.
Agentic Robotics vs. Robot Foundation Models. Robot foundation models aim to acquire reusable
capabilities for robotic tasks through broad training and adaptation, supporting functions ranging from
perception and planning to control [110]. Their capabilities can include reasoning and decision-making:
PaLM-E integrates multimodal observations into embodied planning, while RT-2 connects visual and
linguistic knowledge to action generation and explores intermediate reasoning before action [19, 61]. The
distinction is therefore between the capabilities learned by a model and how those capabilities participate
in an ongoing task. Agentic robotics examines how goals, available actions, and execution feedback govern
subsequent decisions, whether implemented within one model or across several components. Hi Robot
illustrates their compatibility by combining a VLM that updates intermediate instructions with a VLA that
executes them [297].
The following four sections review these domains in turn. Within each domain, we group methods by the
functions they serve and explain their main approaches through representative work.
Table 1. Operational taxonomy of agentic robots. Four functional domains organize seventeen non-exclusive
categories by the part of the robotic system shaped by model-mediated decisions.
Domain
Category
Definition
Model

Designs or revises rewards, objectives, training recipes, or learning
experiments.

Figure 4. Five model-side interventions illustrated by a drawer-retrieval task. Training shapes the learning process;

4 Model: What Should the Agent Learn, Understand, Decide, and
Control?
Figure 4 shows that a drawer task can fail for different reasons, and each calls for a different fix: better
evidence about the handle, a revised order of actions, a feasible grasp command, or a policy that improves
after repeated failures. These are different model-side interventions. Training asks who chooses the
learning process; Perception, Planning, and Robot Control trace how evidence becomes action; Adaptation
asks which parameters or task-specific conditioning change with experience. Their effectiveness depends
on matching each decision to an executor that can carry it out.
4.1 Training
The agent acts on the learning process rather than in the environment. It specifies rewards, objectives,
configurations, or experiments. During this process, an optimizer consumes the resulting artifact, and
robot rollouts reveal whether the policy learned the desired behavior. In iterative workflows, the agent
uses these results to decide what to revise next. Existing systems differ mainly in the scope of this editable
artifact: 1) reward-design methods keep the optimizer fixed and revise only the signal it consumes, whereas
2) program- and experiment-level methods extend revision to policy code, training pipelines, and 3) the
experimental workflow itself.
Reward Design. Reward transfers a behavioral objective into a signal an optimizer can use. Eureka [233]
established the basic loop: a language model writes reward code and revises it using training feedback.
Such feedback, however, can confirm that a terminal predicate is satisfied without revealing whether
execution actually followed the instruction. RDA [161] further addresses this gap with semantic analysis of
the resulting behavior. It decomposes the instruction into subtasks, generates executable reward candidates,
trains policies, and evaluates rollout videos alongside subtask reward logs.
Eureka and RDA evaluate candidate rewards through full policy training, making each evaluation
computationally expensive. Later methods reduce this cost in two ways: they validate rewards with cheaper
evidence, or they narrow what is searched. EvoNav [453] screens reward programs with rules and short
proxy training, assuming that the cheap tests preserve the ranking of useful candidates. Then, CRWM [401]
conditions a one-pass reward-code generator on a causal prior derived from offline reward interventions.
It pays for the prior up front, and RL outcomes never feed back into the reward. Demo2Reward [92]
uses a VLM meta-critic to tune a VLM reward prompt on labelled demonstration prefixes before policy
11


--- PAGE 12 ---
learning. In ReCoVLA [106], a VLM maps failure observations to descriptors, a deterministic compiler
binds them to predefined state-based reward components, and RL trains failure-specific residual policies
while the base VLA stays frozen. Generally, each method trades the generality of full-training feedback
for a cheaper but more constrained source of reliability: proxy rollouts, offline causal structure, labelled
demonstrations, or predefined reward components.
Policy Training. Beyond reward design, agents participate in robot policy development by implementing
policies, organizing training experiments, and revising the learning process. ENPIRE [383] first constructs
reset, verification, and safety interfaces with human feedback, then exposes these interfaces to coding
agents that inspect real rollouts and modify policy or training code. Behavior cloning, reinforcement
learning, and heuristic policy synthesis are alternatives inside the research process, not simultaneous
properties of every resulting controller. HARBOR [191] organizes simulation reinforcement learning into
typed artifacts and gate-checked stages. Failed interface checks return actionable diagnostics; parallel
tuning trials retain code, videos, and logs, and recurring lessons are retrieved for later experiments.
ENPIRE depends on reliable physical resets; HARBOR depends on agreement among generated task,
reward, and algorithm interfaces. Each exposes a different bottleneck to the experimental agent.
This broader search on policy training makes reproducible execution and human control of the research
agenda especially consequential. This work [148] uses coding-agent edits, experiment launches, and
log-based triage for quadruped policy improvement within human-directed research waves. Nautilus [139]
instead standardizes policy, robot, and benchmark contracts and isolates their execution environments,
allowing a coding agent to assemble and validate learning workflows. Overall, these works not only expand
experiment search under a human agenda, but also reduce the integration work required to implement
those experiments.
Workflow. The workflow contains structure and measurements to keep the agent on the right direction.
[417] treat each experiment as a durable transaction with an immutable objective, controller-owned
measurement, artifact-bound tool quality, and crash recovery. In LWD [359], fleet successes, failures, and
human interventions instead update a pretrained generalist VLA through offline-to-online post-training.
The two workflows intervene at different levels: policy post-training from fleet experience and management
of the experiments used to assess revisions.
That separation of decision and measurement also matters when an agent edits more than the training
recipe. Reward, prompt, tool, and data edits can otherwise change both the intervention and its judge.
RoboLineage [231] makes this transition explicit by storing rollouts, reviews, dataset decisions, training
runs, policy metadata, and deployment recommendations as typed artifacts. From the perspective of
foundational infrastructure, FluxVLA Engine [189] supplies configuration-resolved contracts that connect
data, training, evaluation, and deployment. EmbodiedSkills [351] then provides separate interfaces through
which planning, verification, and execution components can be trained or adapted. The useful design
principle is to expose objectives, recipes, and experiments for revision while keeping the measurements
used to judge them stable. The learning choices are analyzed here under Training; the execution contracts
and measurement interfaces that support those choices are discussed as capability-building infrastructure
in Section 7.5. Related works are compared in Table 2.

4.2 Perception
Perception translates observations into task-relevant concepts, relations, geometry, or beliefs. The
agentic intervention may be an interpretation, a request for another observation, or a physical action that
reveals missing evidence. The representation must support the next decision: identifying a handle, for
example, also requires geometry accurate enough for the intended grasp.
Observations. An observations process chooses what evidence to seek when the current view needs
renewal. Situated human-robot conversation systems [160] combine multimodal interaction with task-level
attention and tool calls, while HINT [239] updates intent and routes camera views at manipulation-pattern
transitions, and re-localizes the same committed target when tracking confidence is low. SCOPE [103]
makes camera orientation and zoom explicit actions whose returned observations condition the next
perception request, illustrating a camera agent rather than a fixed detector.
Active perception changes the evidence itself. MG-VQA (formerly Probe) [11] lets a VLM invoke
perception, grasp-and-remove, and pushing tools to expose occluded information before answering a visual
question. [257] uses visual predicate checks and additional viewpoints to correct the state supplied to a
Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) planner. Manipulation can therefore gather information,
while an additional observation can invalidate a symbolic planning premise. The choice to move an object
or camera trades the value of new evidence against motion cost and risk.
Decisions. Based on the previous observation, the decision is the final product that the executor can use.
ABot-N1 [86] uses a recurrent spatial reasoner to update semantic targets while a distinct action expert
supplies continuous navigation behavior; Steerable VLA policies [42] similarly expose semantic-subtask,
atomic-motion, and grounded-point or trace steering modes. A distributed conversational manipulation
framework [142] grounds pixel predictions through depth and robot coordinates on each command,
whereas RoboVista’s [386] robot-VQA abstraction evaluates whether a model can answer perception
questions across diverse application modules. Whether the output is a renewed view, a geometric target, or
a symbolic answer, its quality depends on the downstream decision it must support.
Navigation requires spatial evidence to remain accessible as the robot moves. AgenticNav [188] provides
heading-indexed views, selective recall, a compact trajectory map, and geometric tools. The VLM can
request evidence and choose a navigation target; depth-based validity checks and movement remain below
that choice. SoFar [271] provides an earlier spatial-grounding comparison: object perception constructs a
scene graph containing positions and semantic orientation vectors, and the VLM specifies desired object
geometry that deterministic fitting and motion planning realize. A textual description such as “upright”
becomes useful only when bound to the orientation representation understood by the executor.
13


--- PAGE 14 ---
Reflections. The robot needs a prompt reflection for the change of environment or behavior. [193] first
exposes perception and motion tools through grounded object anchors and execution receipts; the model
receives images and receipts while calibrated depth geometry is handled by the backend. An archived
identity does not make its old pose a valid motion target: lost or invalid anchors require renewed grounding.
Then, [100] use task-conditioned relations and shared instance evidence to request complementary views
before accepting a candidate, but their simulation uses oracle instance masks and IDs. INSPECT [370]
further evaluates learned view utility and commitment decisions through annotated video replay and a
fixed robot-image lattice rather than a closed-loop physical inspection trial.
Navigation summarizes all dynamic and static spatial information. OpenFrontier [261] scores visual
frontiers and verifies candidate targets, returning to search when a candidate is rejected; GoalVLM [127]
combines frontier scoring with allocation across agents and a shared map. FARE [200] reasons over an
evolving belief graph to select global waypoints, while AnchorVLN [330] binds model commands to
typed object handles through a ROS server. Frontier scores help choose where to explore, belief graphs
preserve longer spatial dependencies, and typed handles constrain command references. Their common
vulnerability is stale grounding: a remembered object identity must be re-observed when its location no
longer supports the proposed action. Related works are compared in Table 3.

4.3 Planning
Based on what it perceives, a robot plans a sequence of actions to complete a task. Before carrying out
the plan, it needs to check whether these actions are feasible. The plan may also need to change during
execution, for example when an object moves or an action fails. Planning therefore continues as the
robot carries out the task and learns whether its decisions work. We discuss this process in three stages:
generating a plan, checking it before execution, and updating it during execution.
14


--- PAGE 15 ---
Generating a Plan. Some planners choose one operation at a time and use its result to decide what
to do next. This is useful when later steps depend on information the robot does not yet have. For
example, one system provides tools for looking around, moving to a location, grasping objects, and
accessing memory [290]. The planner reads the text returned by these tools before choosing its next
operation. AgentRob [218] follows a similar approach, with VLM controllers observing the results of
action primitives before selecting the next operation. In both systems, the planner chooses the operation,
and the robot controller handles the physical movement.
The planner can also specify a subgoal and let a trained policy produce the actions needed to achieve it.
Hi Robot [297] uses a high-level VLM to give short language commands to a VLA. These commands are
updated periodically or after user intervention. Gemini Robotics-ER 1.5 [320] similarly handles high-level
reasoning and gives language instructions to the Gemini Robotics 1.5 action model. LocalNav [9] applies
this division to navigation. Its distilled scene-graph reasoner decides whether to move toward an object,
explore a room, perform frontier search, or stop. A PointGoal policy then carries out the navigation.
Beyond choosing the next operation or subgoal, a planner can also specify how several operations depend
on one another. For example, [145] decomposes multi-robot tasks into PDDL subproblems for a classical
planner and uses planning failures to improve the agents’ prompts. In an industrial setting, IMR-LLM [306]
uses an LLM to help construct a disjunctive graph, which a deterministic solver uses to schedule operations.
A process tree then guides the generation of executable robot programs. To connect symbolic planning
with execution, EmboTeam [428] combines semantic processing with PDDL-based search and converts
the resulting plans into behavior trees containing conditions, fallbacks, and synchronization.
Checking a Plan before Execution. Once actions have been proposed, the planner can check whether
they are consistent with its model of the scene and the task requirements. For example, SayPlan [274]
identifies the relevant part of a hierarchical 3D scene graph and uses feedback from a scene-graph
simulator to revise its plan. Within this process, a classical path planner supplies routes between locations.
GAVEL [350] likewise uses a graph world model to verify and repair candidate plans before execution.
For constraints drawn from industrial procedures, MaCoPlanner [389] converts manuals into a typed
intermediate representation and checks action sequences against procedural and state-transition constraints.
Before planning the actions, the system can also check whether the goal is clear and suitable for the robot.
For example, STARS [153] generates candidate goal descriptions and analyzes, repairs, and selects them
before the underlying task-learning agent searches for actions to achieve the selected goal. Here, the checks
concern whether the proposed goal is understandable and appropriate for the agent and its situation, rather
than whether a particular action sequence can be executed.
Alongside checks based on graphs and rules, planners can also use learned models to assess candidate
actions. For instance, Reflective Test-Time Planning [105] scores proposed high-level actions with an
internal reflection model, while external and retrospective reflection later draw on actual outcomes.
Another way to assess actions before execution is to imagine their visual consequences. ReflectVLM [71]
predicts future images for a proposed action sequence and considers these predictions together with the
current and goal images to select the next action primitive. A scripted controller then executes the selected
primitive.
Updating a Plan during Execution. Even after these checks, an action may produce an unexpected result,
or a change in the scene may make the remaining plan unsuitable. The planner therefore needs execution
feedback to decide whether to continue or revise its next steps. For example, WCM [51] revises its next
decision when actual outcomes differ from its predictions, the user changes the instructions, or the scene
changes. When necessary, it can also ask the user for clarification.
To support these decisions, the system needs to report execution results and determine when they call
for replanning. For example, [202] provides status, event, and reflection tools for this purpose. A failed
physical check can serve as a direct trigger. ActionReasoning 2.0 [335] discards the remaining action
sequence when an action fails verification and generates a new sequence from the updated scene state,
retaining previously verified task progress. Similarly, ATP-VPDSG [232] checks the result of each
primitive and uses execution feedback to diagnose failures and revise the remaining actions.
15


--- PAGE 16 ---
When selecting the next action, the planner can also draw on earlier actions and observations rather than
relying only on the latest result. MaP-WAM [448] uses records of completed action segments as planning
context and checks execution progress to determine when to update the plan and its context. For visual
history, ActiveArena-Plan [186] predicts the current subtask and selects historical frames to retain in
memory. A separately trained action policy uses the subtask, selected frames, current observation, and
robot state to generate actions.
For embodied question answering, HFLEX-EQA [270] combines past visual observations with a floorplan
prior and an incrementally constructed scene graph to choose among room exploration, object inspection,
and room discovery. Dedicated low-level planners carry out these operations. Likewise, NavProbe [210]
retrieves historical images and geometric records to revise intermediate goals and assess whether they
have been satisfied before selecting the next navigation action.
However, revising the action sequence may be insufficient if the planning model does not describe the
situation the robot encounters. To address this problem, HUME [382] proposes possible additions to the
domain model, treats them as uncertain hypotheses, and specifies conditions for checking them. Perception
then confirms or rejects these hypotheses, and rejected hypotheses guide subsequent replanning. In this
case, execution feedback changes both the plan and the model used to generate it.
Overall, how far ahead a robot plans should depend on how reliably it can predict the results of its actions.
When those results are predictable, longer plans can organize dependencies across several operations.
When outcomes are uncertain or the scene changes, planning fewer steps ahead allows new observations
to guide subsequent decisions. In either case, checks before execution and feedback during execution help
determine whether to continue with the plan, revise its actions, or update the model used to generate it.
Related works are compared in Table 4.

4.4 Robot Control
Robot control turns the steps chosen during planning into physical movement. How much detail the model
needs to provide depends on what the execution layer can already do. This layer may handle inverse
kinematics, collision checks, and low-level control. Below, we discuss three ways for the model to guide
the robot’s movement: action and language commands, target poses and control programs, and motion
constraints and cost functions. We then discuss how action models, language models, and fixed controllers
handle execution feedback.
Action and Language Commands. A model can control a robot by choosing from a set of predefined
actions. For example, [460] gives navigation agents basic movement commands and evaluates waypoint
tools as an alternative or additional interface. A fixed execution interface carries out the selected movements.
Show-Harness [46] provides commands for translation, rotation, and gripper control, using fixed rules
to convert them into target positions, orientations, and gripper settings. ETA [50] translates commands
shared across robot types into actions supported by each robot. It also requires a new observation after
every tool call that changes the scene and blocks subsequent actions when the grasp or attachment evidence
they require is missing. Demonstrations can also show the model how these commands affect the robot.
RoboDawn [93] uses task demonstrations to guide a VLM’s choice of translation, rotation, and gripper
commands. Even when no task demonstrations are provided, the model still receives examples showing
the effects of the basic commands.
The execution layer can also accept language instructions. For example, [130] searches for instruction
sequences that improve a frozen VLA’s performance and distills them into a language-feedback policy.
During deployment, this policy supplies new instructions only when a calibrated predictor judges that they
are likely to help; otherwise, the VLA keeps the original instruction. ART [412] instead adds reasoning
and tool use to the VLA itself, while retaining a separate output path for continuous robot actions.
Target Poses and Control Programs. When predefined actions or language instructions do not specify
enough spatial detail, a model can provide target positions and orientations directly. For instance,
VIA [107] lets an agent adjust a virtual gripper, preview its pose, and submit it as a waypoint. Screenshots,
coordinates shown when hovering, and pose readouts help the agent choose the target without processing
raw arrays of 3D points. GPT-Policy [53] uses a VLM to request robot movements and gripper operations
in a structured format. A context compiler assembles task references and interaction history for the VLM,
while demonstration actions provide examples for its reasoning. The execution software interpolates
between poses, solves inverse kinematics, sets motion timing, and checks execution. These checks
determine whether the requested motion is kinematically valid and whether the robot has settled near the
target pose. Collision avoidance and task completion require separate checks.
Other systems help the model choose and check motion targets. World Action Agent (WAA) [440]
lets an agent preview and edit proposed movements in a visual workspace, using scene geometry and
motion-planner feedback before execution. Textual procedures and reference images stored as reusable
skills can guide later actions. Robo-Harness K1 [193] provides calibrated spatial measurements and
persistent visual anchors to guide motion requests. Its tools support both bounded incremental movements
and direct moves toward a target, returning achieved motion and residual error after execution.
17


--- PAGE 18 ---
Another approach is to generate motion from templates or keyframes prepared in advance. For example,
ManiSkillFormer [419] uses human-defined skill schemas to guide agents in generating geometric
requirements and motion templates before deployment. During execution, perception supplies the
requested geometry, which the templates use to produce robot waypoints. For whole-body motion,
KINO [39] uses a VLM to select task-relevant keyframes from a predefined library. A retargeter adjusts the
selected keyframes to the current scene, and a keyframe-conditioned policy generates joint-level actions.
A model can also write a program that coordinates several movements and checks their results. ME-
MENTO [314] evolves executable robot policies using rollout feedback and memory-guided program
revision. Its demonstrated policies use finite-state control, grasp and placement checks, and retries to
handle execution failures. FAEA [325] generates Python programs that use APIs to take actions, read
observations, reset the environment, and check success. It uses states and errors observed during simulator
rollouts to revise these programs. Its evaluation provides ground-truth object and gripper states, so
applying the same approach to camera images would require perception to supply the state information
expected by the APIs. In manufacturing, A-RAM [79] uses an LLM to interpret manufacturing goals and
identify which planning variables to explore. Specialized tools then generate G-code and check inverse
kinematics, trajectory timing, and joint motion before execution. During execution, a digital shadow
records robot and extrusion states and compares them with the selected plan.
Motion Constraints and Cost Functions. Instead of specifying a target pose or a sequence of commands,
a model can define requirements for the motion and let a solver find a trajectory. ReKep [120] generates
constraints on relationships between keypoints, both at subgoals and along the path. It tracks these
keypoints as the scene changes, repeatedly optimizes the motion, and supports recovery when a task stage
fails.
VoxPoser [118] writes programs that build spatial value maps for where to move, which regions to avoid,
how to orient the gripper, how fast to move, and when to open or close the gripper. These maps are
updated from new observations during model-predictive control. In both ReKep and VoxPoser, the motion
solver can respond to changes in geometry without calling the language model at every control step.
For manipulation that involves substantial physical contact, CoRAL [469] uses an LLM to generate cost
functions and contact strategies for motion optimization, while a VLM provides initial estimates of physical
parameters. Interaction feedback supports updates to the physical model and revisions to the cost function
or contact strategy. This separates two kinds of adjustment: the solver can find a different motion under
the current requirements, or the model can change those requirements when they prove unsuitable.
Execution Feedback. As the robot moves, the system needs to monitor what happens and decide how to
respond. Some systems process this feedback within the action model, while others send it to a language
model. For example, ARMS [411] adds a visual trigger, robot-state predictions, and a compressed history
of past actions to a VLA. These components help the VLA decide when to act and how to respond, while
perception and memory continue to update during movement. However, remembering past actions does
not guarantee that stored object locations are still correct. If an object moves without being observed, the
robot needs a new observation to update its location.
Feedback can also pass from a motion controller to a language model. COMPASS [329] uses this approach
in simulated multi-agent flocking, separating slower language-model reasoning from faster motion control.
A spatial-transformer controller generates actions and sends learned feedback tokens to the language
model. The language model then uses these tokens to update its reasoning.
Other systems give the language model a more limited role before execution. In one such system, the LLM
interprets the instruction and resolves ambiguity, then a deterministic finite-state controller carries out the
selected operation [104]. The execution layer maps validated tool calls to predefined motion-planning
templates. Before moving the robot, it checks kinematic feasibility and possible collisions. Here, the
described checks take place before movement, while the fixed controller manages execution.
Overall, the model’s output should match what the execution layer can do. For example, if the required
skill is already available, the model may only need to name the action or provide a language instruction.
When these commands are not enough, it can specify target poses, write control programs, or define
18


--- PAGE 19 ---
motion constraints and cost functions. In each case, the system can check whether the robot can carry out
the requested movement before execution. It can then use feedback during and after execution to check
whether the action worked and decide what to do next. Related works are compared in Table 5.
Table 5. Comparison of works on robot control (Section 4.4). Control Output lists the commands or representations
passed to the execution module. Executor identifies the module that turns these outputs into robot actions. Feedback
lists the information returned during execution.

4.5 Adaptation
Adaptation enables robotic systems to improve from experience by modifying inference-time conditioning,
planner configurations, or learned model parameters. These mechanisms differ in their scope and
persistence, but can be combined to support both immediate recovery and long-term improvement.
Experience-Conditioned Decision Making. Prior experience can guide decisions without updating model
parameters. TidyBot [376] infers user preferences from examples, while DROC [430] extracts reusable
knowledge from language corrections. CMMR-VLN [173] converts retrieved navigation experience into
prioritized rules, whereas PhysMem [172] maintains and revises hypotheses about physical interactions.
These methods connect adaptation to memory (Section 7) by conditioning subsequent decisions on stored
experience. Similarly, GPT-Policy [53] conditions a frozen VLM on demonstrations, action references,
and interaction history to support within-task adaptation. However, improvements that depend on retained
context do not necessarily indicate persistent capability acquisition.
Planner Adaptation. Experience can also improve planning without modifying the underlying policy.
SERP [171] combines gradient-based optimization of planner parameters with VLM-guided adjustments
when optimization stalls. Its MARS extension further retrieves prior recovery experience to inform
subsequent configurations. Such methods primarily address failures in planning and search rather than
limitations in the policy’s learned capabilities.
Policy and Model Updates. Parameter updates enable more persistent behavioral changes. OLAF [209]
converts verbal corrections into revised action labels for visuomotor policy training. Yell At Your
19


--- PAGE 20 ---
Robot [296] instead updates a high-level policy while keeping the low-level controller fixed. Uncertainty-
Aware Policy Steering [421] separates instruction clarification from residual policy learning using
corrective demonstrations. These approaches target different sources of error, including task interpretation,
action selection, and execution.
Autonomous interaction provides an additional learning signal. Self-Improving Embodied Foundation
Models [289] uses a frozen supervised checkpoint for progress rewards and success detection while
optimizing a separate policy with reinforcement learning. Its physical experiments use LanguageTable,
whereas ALOHA self-improvement is evaluated in simulation. Language Model Predictive Control [197]
fine-tunes a code-generating LLM through simulated teaching interactions. Reflective Test-Time Plan-
ning [105] updates high-level action selection and reflection from retrospective evaluations while keeping
the external evaluator fixed. These methods illustrate how learning can target distinct components under
different supervision mechanisms.
Learning across Tasks and Deployments. Experience collected during execution can support subsequent
training. PAFF [80] relabels exploratory trajectories with language instructions, while RoboCat [18]
incorporates data generated by task-adapted policies into successive generalist training iterations. VER-
ITAS [437] selects verified successful executions, whereas HERO [174] combines heuristic execution
and exemplar reuse before policy training. VLAW [95] iteratively improves a world model using real
interactions and filters generated trajectories for policy updates. At deployment scale, RECAP [268] and
Learning While Deploying [359] leverage autonomous experience and human corrections to improve poli-
cies. These approaches connect adaptation to data collection and selection (Section 5), with performance
depending on supervision quality, experience diversity, and interaction costs.
The choice of adaptation mechanism depends on the source of failure. Perception errors may require
additional observations, planning errors may require replanning, and persistent execution failures may
motivate policy updates. Evaluation should distinguish within-episode recovery, cross-task generalization,
and improvements following retraining, while assessing whether previously acquired capabilities are
preserved. Related works are compared in Table 6.
Table 6. Comparison of works on adaptation (Section 4.5). Demonstrations include task examples and action
trajectories; Corrections include corrective instructions and interventions. Execution Outcomes include success,
failure, and execution feedback.

5 Data: How Should the Agent Collect, Annotate, and Refine Robot
Data?
The progress of agentic robots is largely driven by data processing pipelines for robot learning. Modern
agent-centric methods actively collect interactive data, interpret robot demonstration behaviors, and
refine supervision signals from real-world execution feedback. This section decomposes agent-oriented
data operations into three key stages: collection, annotation, and refinement. Data collection obtains
agent-explored interactive trajectories and robot self-demonstration datasets from robot task rollouts. Data
annotation augments raw robot data with structured supervision for task understanding, spatial grounding,
and so on. Data refinement improves data quality by removing noisy samples and correcting inaccurate
supervision. Together, these three stages form a complete data optimization workflow for agentic robot
learning. The following sections elaborate on each stage and review representative technical approaches.

Figure 5. Three data-side interventions over shared robot experience, illustrated by a drawer interaction with failure
and recovery. Collection acquires or generates experience; Annotation adds task meaning, temporal structure, and
supervision; and Refinement selects useful records or segments and revises unreliable supervision.
5.1 Collection
Agent-based data collection aims to generate new robot trajectories or demonstrations through autonomous
agents. We group these methods into three categories. Physical Data Collection gathers data from
real-robot interaction and human demonstration. Simulation Data Generation produces trajectories by
running tasks, policies, or programs within a simulator. Generative Data Synthesis uses generative models
to create artificial observations and motions.
Physical Data Collection. An early example, AutoRT [3], uses language models to propose collection
tasks drawing upon existing visual scene descriptions and the capabilities of available collection policies.
The selected tasks are then attempted through scripted picking, RT-2 [19], or human teleoperation to
21


--- PAGE 22 ---
collect real-world trajectories. SOAR [467] uses CogVLM [352] to identify tasks that can be performed
in the current scene, then prioritizes less-attempted tasks to collect data across a wider range of tasks.
Sustained agent-based data collection involves key design choices for task execution, scene reset, and human
intervention. Zero2Skill [334] leverages Seed1.8 [22] to process images captured after data-collection and
scene-reset stages. Based on the resulting trial-outcome evaluation, the data-collection loop either proceeds
onward or retries the ongoing trial, with human assistance invoked once retries reach the predefined limit.
RoboClaw [178] combines visual observations with task progress in a VLM controller, which invokes a
paired reset policy after confirming task completion.
Agents can also direct additional data collection toward underrepresented tasks and weaknesses revealed by
robot execution. Tether [199] prioritizes tasks lacking sufficient successful demonstrations and generates
scene-adapted preparatory actions. In its early collection experiments, the pipeline used Gemini 2.5
Flash [56] to plan task sequences for scene preparation leading to the target collection task, while GPT-4.1
judged task success. Success evaluation compared camera views before and after execution, with an
additional check matching the source demonstration’s final keypoint to the gripper’s final image position.
The paper later adopts Gemini Robotics-ER 1.5 for both task selection and success evaluation, and
reports a post-hoc evaluation without the additional correspondence check. RoboLineage [231] reviews
recorded video segments and keyframes, together with operator notes and robot logs, to identify where
task execution fails. It combines these findings with data-coverage and policy-evaluation summaries to
recommend additional collection for weak task phases. FIND [68] also uses a VLM to judge each attempt
as successful or unsuccessful from the task instruction and before-and-after scene images. UPS [421]
requests human corrections when its verifier determines the current policy cannot complete the task.
Simulation Data Generation. RoboGen [362] is an early example that introduces a robotic agent that
proposes tasks, constructs simulation environments, and generates training supervision. Specifically, the
agent decomposes the task into subtasks, selects and arranges the required objects in Genesis [82], and uses
methods such as motion planning to generate robot behavior in simulation. To generate tasks grounded in
everyday human activities, Video2Policy [409] uses internet videos as the source of task specifications.
It reconstructs the manipulated objects and estimates their poses, then uses a vision-language model to
generate executable task code for Isaac Gym [236]. GenSim2 [113] uses an agent to create manipulation
tasks and guide motion planning, then executes the motions in SAPIEN [381] to collect robot trajectories.
RoboFactory [273] focuses on collaboration between multiple robots, using a language-model planner to
assign subtasks and coordinate their execution in ManiSkill [318].
More recent work broadens the types of simulation data collected and gives agents greater responsibility
for the collection process. RoboTalk [84] generates communication messages alongside joint robot actions,
validates their sequences, and replays them in RoboCasa365 [246] to collect the corresponding visual
observations. ARSTAG [169] and EmbodiedSWE-Gen [294] automate the preparation and expansion
of collection programs. Policy failures can also guide what the agent collects next. EmbodiRSI [158]
monitors policy execution in Isaac Sim and uses the observed failures to generate recovery trajectories and
collect additional data in difficult scene configurations. The policy is then updated and evaluated again,
allowing the agent to adjust subsequent collection to the robot’s remaining weaknesses.
Generative Data Synthesis. Unlike previous methods like RoboTransfer [214], Real2Edit2Real [451],
RoboVIP [333], and RoboDream [407] that synthesize robot data through predefined generation and
trajectory-processing pipelines, agent-based methods use large language models or multi-modal large
language models to decide what data to generate and coordinate the generation process. RoboCurate [152]
leverages Gemini 3 Pro to derive manipulation tasks from visual scene observations and produce prompts
for video generation. Additionally, it checks whether simulator replays of the predicted actions are
consistent with the motion in the corresponding generated videos. V-Dreamer [101] uses Qwen-Max [4]
to turn a scene description into a list of object descriptions, which guides 3D asset generation and scene
construction. For long instructions, a foundation model divides the task into subtasks that are processed
in sequence. A video model generates a manipulation video from an image of the simulated scene and
the current subtask instruction. The pipeline tracks points on the target object across video frames and
reconstructs their three-dimensional motion. It combines this motion with a generated grasp pose to
22


--- PAGE 23 ---
compute a robot trajectory, then executes the trajectory in Genesis [82] to collect demonstrations for policy
training. Wh0 [45] uses two agents to generate manipulation instructions: one expands object and attribute
vocabulary, while the other selects less-used words based on usage counts. Qwen3-VL describes the
required hand and object motions to guide human-hand video generation from edited workspace images.
Wrist poses and finger-joint movements recovered from these videos provide action labels, which are
combined with real robot demonstrations to train a dexterous policy. These collection routes concern
experience records. RATs [435] and Teach and Grow [249] instead primarily retain programs, lessons, or
behavior-library entries. Their practice-to-skill workflows are therefore discussed in Section 7.6, which
examines the construction, validation, and reuse of procedural artifacts. Related works are compared in
Table 7.

5.2 Annotation
While data collection yields videos paired with robot-state sequences, such records are often deficient
in textual descriptions, spatial markers, interaction targets, and outcome annotations. To remedy this
gap, agent-driven annotation employs intelligent agents to interpret raw records, orchestrate annotation
utilities, and produce as well as validate the missing labels. We categorize existing agent-based annotation
approaches into four groups: text annotation, spatial annotation, interaction annotation, and outcome
annotation, based on their respective annotation objectives.
Text Annotation. The most common agent-based text annotation is to produce subtask descriptions and
video captions that mark task stages and observable events, respectively. For example, NILS [16] generates
task-level language instructions for robot video segments. Specifically, it detects segment boundaries
from object movements, state changes, and gripper interactions, then uses Gemini Pro to describe the
manipulation stage performed in each segment. IVLR [211] generates subtask-level video captions. It
divides a robot trajectory into stages, then queries Qwen3-VL to describe the detailed gripper-object
interaction or spatial relation in each stage. EgoTools [322] adopts Gemini 3 Flash to caption consecutive
video segments, propagating prior captions to subsequent segments for improved temporal consistency.
23


--- PAGE 24 ---
Another form of text annotation adopts question-answer (QA) pairs to capture scene properties, action
sequences, and fine-grained execution details.
For example, RoboAnnotatorX [154] uses GPT-4o
to generate QA about the objects present, their spatial relationships, and the order of robot actions,
using scene descriptions and timestamped action annotations as the source information. Meanwhile,
AnnotateAnything [227] creates robot-oriented QA pairs from simulation trajectory logs. A VLM
varies question phrasing for target objects, interacting parts, approach directions and execution outcomes,
with answers sourced from simulator records. REAL [241] and RoboTalk [84] use Gemini models to
generate dialogue annotations alongside robot actions, covering clarification of ambiguous instructions
and communication between cooperating robots. Methods such as SpatialVLM [33], SpaceVista [311],
and Exemplar2VQA [415] combine visual models or coding agents with 3D geometry to generate
question-answer pairs about object positions, distances, and spatial relationships. RoboSpatial [301]
derives spatial answers from 3D geometry, projection, and ray casting, and constructs question-answer
pairs with deterministic templates.
Beyond question-answer pairs, reasoning-trace annotations augment recorded behaviors with high-level
plans and explanatory rationales. Specifically, ECoT [425] and TIC-VLA [124] use Gemini 1.0 and
GPT-5, respectively, to generate reasoning traces that explain the robot’s current subtask and next action in
manipulation and navigation tasks. Along this line, ERVLA [310] utilizes Qwen3.5-397B to derive shared
goals and subtask sequences per episode, alongside fine-grained reasoning for selected timesteps.
Spatial Annotation. Robotic agents perform spatial annotation by interpreting demonstrations, invoking
perception tools, and verifying spatial labels. NILS [16] and FSD [424] use language-model-guided
pipelines to identify objects for annotation. NILS uses Grounding DINO [216] to obtain object bounding
boxes and DEVA [54] to track object masks across video frames. FSD uses Grounded SAM [281] to
obtain bounding boxes and segmentation masks. AnnotateAnything [227] and EmbodiedGen V2 [357]
combine 3D segmentation with VLM-based interpretation to annotate object parts, with EmbodiedGen
V2 additionally checking and refining the resulting labels. SPARC [17] and Keypose Exploration [229]
interpret interaction events through language models and align them with visual tracks or robot states to
annotate trajectories and keyposes. AutoFocus-IL [85] and Workspace Models [57] identify task-relevant
objects and events through VLM reasoning and localize them to generate spatial saliency annotations.
Interaction Annotation. Interaction annotation captures physical engagements between hands, robotic
grippers, tools and target objects, covering typical behaviors such as touching, grasping, pushing, and
pressing. The annotation pipeline generally consists of reasoning over interaction intent and retrieving
contact-related evidence via external tools. For affordance annotation, several representative works,
such as AFUN [364], EgoAfford [91], Affordance2Action [213], A4-Agent [444], and Affordance
Agent Harness [114] integrate model reasoning with tool-aided localization. In these works, agents
or LLM-driven workflows first identify object regions suitable for the desired manipulation, then call
detection and segmentation modules to pinpoint these functional areas. For dexterous hands, DextER [162]
and OmniDexVLG [436] combine model reasoning with tool-derived contact evidence to annotate finger-
and link-level contacts with object parts and interpret their grasp functions.
Outcome Annotation. Outcome annotation labels task achievement in robot executions, including
intermediate progress, final success or failure, and safety violations. Agents or model-guided workflows
interpret evaluation criteria and inspect execution evidence to produce these labels. For task achievement,
RoboReward [163] and Zero2Skill [334] use visual-language judgments against explicit task criteria to
assign completion scores or success labels. For intermediate progress, ROVER [288] and ProcVLM [70]
organize visual reasoning around subtasks to annotate their completion states and estimate task progress.
ARS [109] further uses agents to invoke video-inspection tools, verify proposed task events, and revise
frame-level progress annotations. For safety, ShieldVLA [319] uses an agentic workflow to refine safety
criteria from misranked examples and generate calibrated frame-level risk labels. Related works are
compared in Table 8.
24


--- PAGE 25 ---

5.3 Refinement
Data refinement improves the suitability of recorded robot demonstrations through data selection and
supervision revision. Agent-based systems combine model reasoning with execution evidence to determine
which trajectories or segments to retain and whether earlier annotations need correction.
RoboLineage [231] and Zero2Skill [334] use model-assisted review to determine whether recorded
trajectories meet the criteria for training. Selection can also operate within a trajectory: HELP [431] and
ARS [109] use estimated task progress to retain useful segments while excluding unproductive actions,
allowing parts of unsuccessful attempts to support learning. HELP relies on its VLAC-CUT critic to

scene from observations. Task Generation defines goals, constraints, and success conditions. Benchmarking fixes
test conditions, reset protocols, metrics, and evaluators for success, safety, efficiency, and robustness.
distinguish progress and recovery from idle or failure-inducing behavior, while ARS uses agents to call
video-inspection tools and verify task events before progress estimates guide segment selection.
Zero2Skill [334] and Reflective Test-Time Planning [105] revisit earlier supervision when feedback
changes how an execution should be judged. Zero2Skill applies human-corrected success criteria to
previously collected demonstrations and updates their labels. Reflective Test-Time Planning uses later
execution outcomes to revise earlier action evaluations, retaining the updated reflections and scores as
supervision for subsequent learning. Related works are compared in Table 9.
