6 Environment: What Worlds, Tasks, and Tests Should the Agent
Build?
The Environment branch covers the world, task, and evaluator that shape robot interaction. Reconstruction,
Task Generation, and Benchmarking address these three objects (Figure 6). They can fail independently: a
scene may be unstable, a task may be infeasible, or an evaluator may say little about the ability under test.
In a drawer task, the scene must allow the joint motion, the task must identify the object to retrieve, and
the evaluator must check the final relation. The construction loop should link each failed check to the
object that needs revision.
6.1 Reconstruction
Reconstruction turns observations, demonstrations, or a requested layout into an executable scene. It must
recover the structure that a task uses, the changes caused by interaction, and an interface through which an
26


--- PAGE 27 ---
agent can inspect and revise the result. Checks should expose errors that matter for execution rather than
reward visual similarity alone.
Static Structure. Static structure includes objects, poses, supports, and the properties that make a scene
stable. ARSTAG [169] reconstructs instruction-relevant objects and support relations, links identities to
segmentation masks, and repairs poses before collecting demonstrations. SimFoundry [275] decomposes
video into meshes and articulated parts, stabilizes the scene, and creates digital-cousin variants; Genie
Sim 3.0 [414] assembles scenes from an asset library. RoboLab [398] returns displacement errors from
stability simulation to the generator, while RoboPlayground [360] settles a goal configuration and repairs
task code when its success predicate fails. GIF [393] checks the requested relation after settling objects
before generating demonstrations. These checks keep reconstruction tied to support, contact, and task
feasibility.
Dynamic and Articulated Structure. A replayable scene must also respond correctly to new actions.
Agentic Real2Sim [36] identifies task-relevant objects, reconstructs their meshes, and tracks 6-DoF poses,
with optional VLM refinement. Video2World [316] evaluates coding agents that recover a scene and a
control program from embodied video, while 4DCodeBench [292] tests code-based dynamic reconstruction
with analytic motion, keyframes, or simulation. Matching observed motion does not establish correct
response to a new action, so these are separate checks.
Articulated assets require kinematic tests. Articulate-Anything [159] renders generated link and joint
code, compares it with the input, and exports a URDF after revision. Articraft [461] specifies parts, joints,
and tests in code, using compilation and geometric diagnostics to guide repair. Deformable assets need
different representations: CoDimRecon [387] tests curves, surfaces, and volumes through simulation;
DiagGen [35] routes diagnostic failures to segmentation, material, or mesh repair; and DexAgent [361]
selects reconstruction skills and verifiers for rigid, articulated, or deformable objects before generating
trajectories and training data. These procedures establish simulated behavior and task feasibility, not
real-world validity.
Construction Interfaces and Revision. The interface determines which scene changes an agent can
make. SceneSmith [266] edits structured scene objects through designer, critic, and orchestrator roles
with rollback. SAGE [379] exposes placement, motion, and deletion operations and rejects or repairs
unstable arrangements. EmbodiedGen V2 [357] provides typed scene-graph operations over a persistent
world state. Code interfaces allow more expressive changes: ChronoAgentic [338] generates and repairs
PyChrono code; SimWorld Studio’s SimCoder [144] uses compilation, collision, physics, and visual
feedback; LEGO-Anything [183] rebuilds an image in Blender through repeated code execution; and
Code4Scene [410] checks Unreal scene edits for support, penetration, and navigation utility. A constrained
API narrows the error space, whereas generated code needs software, geometric, and physical checks.
Revision should match the intended downstream use. RAPID [222] screens perturbed simulator variants
with agent-written feasibility filters and keeps the resulting primitive and strategy code fixed at deployment.
Awomo-SimDataEngine [6] builds worlds in stages with acceptance checks and dependency-aware repair.
DexSim2Real [429] uses a VLM realism critic and an LLM-derived skill curriculum to tune simulation
randomization for sim-to-real transfer. Appearance and stability checks cannot replace contact, friction,
reachability, or downstream execution tests. Related works are compared in Table 10.
Table 10. Comparison of works on reconstruction (Section 6.1). Selected inputs and representations may co-occur.
Scene includes graphs and layouts; Params.: parameters. Engines lists construction or verification software,
excluding export-only targets. Input: Ú Text;

6.2 Task Generation
Task generation specifies the goal, initial state, constraints, and success conditions that a robot must
execute. It covers both construction of executable tasks and adjustment of task distributions as the learner
changes.
Executable Task Construction. GenSim [345] generates task libraries as simulation code. RoboGen [362]
couples task proposals with scenes and supervision, while Video2Policy [409] derives task code and
rewards from internet video. RoboLab [398] composes tasks from competency axes, predicates, and
asset constraints, then feeds syntax and asset errors back to the generator. HARBOR [191] checks
scene stages, initialization, termination, actions, and observations. Scene2Demo [219] links subtasks
through an object–action graph and generates executable initial and goal conditions. PARTNR [31] filters
generated instructions and initial states and builds evaluators for propositions, dependencies, and temporal
constraints.
Checks can also govern generation of human interaction and physical feasibility. FATE [366] separates
static semantic, geometric, and precondition checks from dynamic execution checks and repairs the failing
component. GenPHRI [343] uses a text2sim2real pipeline to generate a human, a scene, and an assistive
task; collision and kinematic checks constrain the resulting placements and motions. These repairs can
change task difficulty or meaning, so feasibility and objective preservation should be checked together.
Generating a solution does not necessarily generate a new task. EmbodiedSmith [272] composes skills
into task graphs and revises plans and initial placements while keeping assets, layout, and success criteria
fixed. Scene2Demo likewise revises action flows from rollout critiques and restarts the current subtask
from a saved state. The resulting demonstrations belong to data generation (Section 5.1), not to a new task
definition.
Adaptive Task Distributions. CurricuLLM [286] generates a subtask sequence, candidate reward and
goal-distribution code, and passes the selected policy to the next subtask. RATs [435] selects practice
28


--- PAGE 29 ---
tasks from scene information, a code-skill library, and failure memory, balancing novelty and difficulty.
SimWorld Studio [144] instead feeds embodied-agent performance to an environment generator that
changes environments and task difficulty. RATs changes the next goal; SimWorld Studio changes the task
distribution. Evaluation on separately specified tasks is needed because a curriculum optimized from the
learner’s own success signal can become easier without improving the target ability. Related works are
compared in Table 11.
Table 11. Comparison of works on task generation (Section 6.2). Output lists generated or revised artifacts. Eval.:
success/evaluation code; Config.: initialization/randomization settings; Plan: executable solution, which may
preserve the task. UE5: Unreal Engine 5; MolmoSp.: MolmoSpaces. Multiple (HARBOR): Isaac Lab, ManiSkill,
Genesis, MJLab.

6.3 Benchmarking
Benchmarking fixes the task, interface, reset protocol, and scoring rule used to measure an ability. Agents
may be the systems under test, or they may construct and revise the evaluation itself. The same task can
measure different abilities when observation access, execution assistance, feedback timing, or interaction
budgets change.
Evaluating Agents in Robot Control. Planning benchmarks test whether an agent converts an instruction
into robot operations. EmbodiedBench [399] varies action abstraction and returns environment feedback.
Embodied Agent Arena [115] uses multi-round interaction in its Planning and Manipulation domains.
MissionBench [247] evaluates an MLLM that selects motion primitives, magnitudes, and stopping
decisions for aerial missions. KinDER [122] evaluates open-loop composition of parameterized skills, so
its score does not measure feedback-driven replanning.
Tool and programming benchmarks evaluate executable decisions. EmbodiedToolBench [466] separates
tool recognition, selection, execution, output use, and tool-chain composition.
CaP-X [74] varies
state access, API abstraction, examples, and visual grounding while executing generated control code.
EmbodiedSWE [294] gives coding agents simulator state, rendering, and execution feedback, then grades
the resulting program in a separate environment.
Existing robot suites can host these evaluations. LIBERO [206] provides a standard lifelong-learning suite,
and LIBERO-PRO [465] changes the protocol to reduce memorization and improve robustness. CaP-X
evaluates CaP-Agent0 on LIBERO-PRO with position and instruction perturbations, while ASPIRE [228]
uses LIBERO-PRO, robosuite, and BEHAVIOR-1K with separate protocols for fixed programs and
29


--- PAGE 30 ---
trace-based code revision. The agent score therefore depends on whether the program is fixed before
evaluation or revised from the current trace.
Harness VLA [441] tests agent selection of analytic primitives with a frozen VLA on LIBERO, LIBERO-
PRO, RoboCasa365, and RoboTwin C2R. RoboHarness [116] tests heterogeneous policy orchestration,
perturbation robustness, and long-horizon execution on LIBERO variants and adds tasks for policy
handoffs. In both cases, the task suite alone does not determine the measured ability; the execution and
adaptation protocol does as well. RoboDojo [41] combines 42 simulation tasks with 18 real-world tasks
and evaluates generalization, memory, precision, long-horizon execution, and open-vocabulary instruction
following. RobotWorld [404] extends this setting across manipulation, mobile manipulation, locomotion,
driving, and aerial control. Its 84 simulation tasks specify interaction budgets and executable success
checks, and its trace analysis links outcomes to the agent’s perception, computation, feedback use, and
completion decisions.
Active-perception tests evaluate information gathering. VABench [443] asks an MLLM to inspect a
scene, issue camera and Cartesian commands, and revise decisions after new observations. ActiveArena-
Plan [186] uses a planner to select subtasks and history for a VLA executor. The former tests observation
gathering directly; the latter also tests how selected history guides task progress.
Memory tests ask whether earlier observations remain useful. RoboGraph [346] controls dependencies,
observation access, and external events to test state maintenance and updating. RoboMemArena [164] uses
a VLM planner to select subtasks and retained observations for a low-level executor. EvoNav-Bench [355]
introduces hidden scene changes that can make retained evidence stale. MEMOBench [308] adds
executable checkpoints for memory storage, update, and compression, separating memory fidelity from
final manipulation success. Although its reported subjects are VLA policies, the same process-level checks
can be applied to agent-controlled rollouts; memory mechanisms are discussed in Section 7.
Safety tests separate hazard avoidance from task completion. ReactHuman [190] scores an MLLM’s
motor plan for a sudden event against simulator ground truth. ManiGuard [264] uses specification-
grounded runtime monitors and reports safety separately from task success. Its published evaluations
use VLA policies, but the monitors can also score agent-controlled rollouts under the same task protocol.
ReactHuman pauses simulation during inference, so it measures the hazard-response decision rather than
real-time reaction.
A benchmark score therefore depends on the full execution contract: agent decision, executor, feedback
path, observation access, controller assistance, and interaction budget.
Agent-Directed Evaluation Construction. An agent that builds an evaluation must specify the ability
under test, the task conditions, and the evidence used for scoring. ManipEvalAgent [49] decomposes
an evaluation request, generates tasks and evaluation tools, and uses rollout results to propose further
tests or summarize the tested policy. Embodied-BenchForge [133] generates task templates, data, and
executable verifiers; interactive tasks require restorable states and terminal checks, while offline tasks
require grounded evidence and executable scoring. Its dependency graph supports targeted rollback and
repair. A2Eval [438] groups embodied-reasoning questions by capability, generates inference and scoring
code, and repairs that code from execution feedback.
Adaptive evaluation can reveal weaknesses, but its conclusions depend on test selection, access to prior
results, and score validation. Fixing test conditions independently of the evaluated system supports broader
claims. Improving the evaluator and improving the robot are separate outcomes.
World construction, task generation, and benchmarking diagnose different failures: unstable geometry
requires scene repair, an infeasible goal requires task revision, and an invalid success signal requires
evaluator repair. Keeping these checks separate makes failures easier to locate and simulated conditions
easier to validate on hardware. Related works are compared in Table 12.
30


--- PAGE 31 ---

7 Harness: How Should Robot Capabilities Be Orchestrated,
Recovered, and Evolved?
A robot may have capable models and policies yet still fail if they are poorly organized around execution.
We use the harness to denote the software that makes agentic capabilities available for robot use, by
managing how components are invoked, what information they receive, how execution is checked, and
what can be reused later. This section organizes harness functions into six components, as shown in
Figure 7. Orchestration governs calls and handoffs between components. Memory supplies information
retained from earlier steps. Monitoring and Recovery assess execution and respond to failure. Skills
package reusable behavior. Infrastructure exposes and gates executable capabilities. Layered Systems
divide authority across planners, learned policies, and analytic control. The first three components act
within a single attempt, Skills carry behavior across attempts, and the last two describe the software and
system structure beneath them.
31


--- PAGE 32 ---

Figure 7. Harness functions that organize agentic capabilities around robot execution. The central row connects
task goals and evidence to decision and dispatch, exposed executors, robot execution, and returned feedback.
Orchestration governs calls and handoffs; Memory supplies retained information; Monitoring and Recovery assess
execution and respond to failure; Skills package reusable behavior; Infrastructure exposes and gates callable
capabilities; and Layered Systems divide authority and operating rates across planners, learned policies, and analytic
control.
7.1 Orchestration
Orchestration decides which model, policy, skill, or tool runs, how long it keeps control, and how control
passes to the next component. We illustrate orchestration by following a single handoff order through four
steps, where the harness chooses the next executor, defines what the function calling takes and returns,
makes sure the next executor can start from the resulting state, and decides when control comes back.
Routing and Dispatch. In an agentic harness, the planner rarely acts on the robot directly. Instead, it
decides which executor should handle the next step, such as a learned policy, an analytic tool, or a human,
and passes the task to it. A key design choice is when this decision is made. VoLo [40] makes it during
execution. It monitors a running VLA and, when the action goes wrong, halts the VLA and switches to a
hand-designed skill guided by perception. MistyPilot [354] makes it in advance. It generates persistent
bindings between sensor events and skills for social-robot behavior, so that later events trigger skills
without a new planning step. AeroWeaver [226] makes it at the level of the team, assigning body-specific
aerial skills to distributed roles in a simulated swarm. M2HRI [99] further includes humans among the
parties to route to, adding a coordinator for human participation and an interaction planner conditioned on
memory. Together, these systems place the routing decision at different points, from a running action to a
standing rule to a team-level role.
Meanwhile, routing is reliable only when the dispatcher knows what each executor can and cannot do. This
knowledge can come from a description of the robot’s body or from the results of earlier calls. EMOS [37]
takes the first approach for teams of different robots. It builds a Robot Resume for each robot from its
URDF model and forward-kinematics tools, and each robot’s agent uses this resume to assess an assigned
subtask and report whether it can perform it before calling its skill library. Harness VLA [441] and
Physical Agency [75] take the second approach on a single robot, placing analytic tools and a VLA behind
one dispatch interface and choosing the next executor according to the evidence each call returns, such
as grasp checks and typed failure messages. Guava [208] applies the same idea across levels of control,
choosing between semantic operations, such as grasping and alignment, and numerical motion tools based
on visual observations, the robot’s state, and earlier tool outcomes.
32


--- PAGE 33 ---
Function Protocol. Robot capabilities are usually exposed to the agent as functions, which the agent
calls with a name and some arguments without seeing how they work inside. A function protocol fills this
gap by stating how a function should be called, what it reports back, and when a call counts as successful.
The first of these defines what counts as a valid call, and can be written for the agent to follow or enforced
by the harness. EmbodiedTool [466] writes it for the agent, giving each tool a typed description of its
inputs, outputs, and usage conditions that the agent’s call must match. ROSClaw [26] enforces it in the
harness, checking each call to a physical TurtleBot3 or Go2 robot against velocity limits and a list of
allowed interfaces, so that an invalid call is stopped even if the agent produces it.
Once a call has run, the protocol defines what comes back to the agent. A simple success flag says little
about the robot’s situation, so some protocols return more. Physical Agentic AI [220] returns each skill
outcome with the robot state at that moment, which later dispatch can use without calling the foundation
model again. Physical Agency [75] returns typed failures, so that the agent can respond to each kind
differently, for example by setting up an operation again or switching to another executor.
Even a detailed result is still the executor’s own report. Success conditions add an independent check by
stating what should be true after a call succeeds. Thea [348] attaches such conditions to each tool, and a
task evaluator separate from the planner checks them against current observations.
State Connection. Each executor works reliably only from certain starting states, so a correctly chosen
executor can still fail if the previous step leaves the robot elsewhere. State connection makes sure that the
state left by one executor connects to the state the next one needs. Existing systems make this connection
in four ways. The first two ways adjust either the state or the executor. RoboHarness [116] adjusts the state
to fit the executor. It retrieves past trajectories from memory and uses a motion-planned memory bridge to
move the robot to the starting state expected by the next policy. RoboRouter [48] adjusts the executor to fit
the state. It selects a policy that suits the current state from several candidates, and uses summaries of past
runs to improve later selections.
The other two ways set up the connection before the handoff, which is common when several robots
share a task. In this case, the next robot must also know what the others have done and which resources
it may use. Some systems check whether the successor is ready. Some systems check this before each
handoff. RoCo [237] lets robots agree on a plan through dialogue, then checks whether the plan is feasible
before they move. RoboTalk [84] checks each message and joint action against symbolic preconditions
and resource ownership, so that a robot acts only when what it was told and what it may use are consistent.
Other systems fix the state connection structure in advance. AGOS-Agent [416] divides a task into search
for candidates, route planning on the ground, and verification from ground-level views, so that the input
each stage receives is set at design time. Together, these approaches either repair a mismatch when it
appears or prevent it by checking or fixing the handoff ahead of time.
Handoff Timing. Handoff timing decides when control returns from an executor to the decision process.
Returning control too late lets a failing action continue, while returning it too early interrupts an action that
would have succeeded. Existing systems differ mainly in the signal that triggers this return, which can be
task progress, an execution budget, or a periodic or event-driven schedule. Progress-based triggers return
control when the observed state of the task changes. HarnessWAM [89] uses a fast progress estimator to
detect such changes, and a VLM task manager then decides whether to move to the next subtask, gather
new observations, replan, or reset the robot. Cortex [263] checks visual progress continuously while the
robot moves, and keeps a running semantic memory to decide whether the current stage should continue.
Other systems limit each call in time or coordinate components that run at different rates. VLAs-as-
Tools [166] runs the VLA as a tool with a limited execution budget, during which the VLA reports
progress to an outside monitor that can move on or replan. Retriever [446] runs perception, belief updates,
planning, the VLA, and control asynchronously, with separate periodic or event-driven clocks and explicit
input-synchronization and commit rules that preserve causality. Generally, these triggers of handoff timing
differ in what the harness waits for before taking control back, whether an observed change in progress,
the end of a budget, or the next tick of a clock. Related works are compared in Table 13.

7.2 Memory
Memory supplies information from earlier steps to later decisions without changing model weights. Unlike
reusable behavior such as rules or programs, memory records what has happened, including what the
robot has tried, what it has observed, and how far the task has progressed. We group existing methods into
three categories according to the stage of memory they address. Memory Writing decides what is recorded
and when. Memory Updating keeps records consistent with a changing scene. Memory Retrieval selects
records for the next decision and reuses earlier solutions.
Memory Writing. Memory writing decides which parts of an episode are worth recording. Common
records include failed choices, task progress, and observed experience. Some systems record failures so
that the robot can return to them later. HAM-VLN [205] stores each failure together with the place where
it happened, which supports backtracking and verified stopping during navigation. Robo-Cortex [30]
checks imagined candidate actions against fresh real observations and keeps the results in a memory with
two levels of detail. Other systems record task progress. HiMe [131] keeps a subtask list with a status
for each item to guide planning, separate from an editable record of the episode, and uses a separate
checker to judge recent visual progress. A third group records broader experience. MEMORA [420]
stores experience as records of places, objects, activities, and inferred knowledge, which are edited during
the task and merged afterward. ABot-AgentOS [321] stores such records as graphs linked to their sources
rather than as raw history.
Beyond what to record, a harness must also decide when to write and in what form. Writing after every step
34


--- PAGE 35 ---
fills memory with redundant records, while writing too rarely misses events that later decisions depend on.
MessyMem [8] writes after each physical interaction. A VLM reviews the action, notes outcomes such as
a missed grasp or a locked drawer, and updates the matching object entry with these notes and key images.
Skills in Weights, Memory in Code [452] instead treats the memory strategy itself as code, developing it
from rollout traces and keeping it as a runnable program. These systems thus differ in whether writing is
triggered by events or governed by a learned program.
Memory Updating. Memory updating keeps stored records consistent with a changing scene. As objects
move and earlier observations become outdated, a memory entry that was correct when written can later
be wrong. Existing systems handle this in three ways, by keeping different kinds of records separate, by
refreshing records regularly, or by checking a record before relying on it. Mimir [392] keeps task memory
separate from world memory, and updates execution progress, failed hypotheses, object relations, and
evidence with different operators. RoboStream [123] refreshes its evidence about the robot and the scene
at every planning step. OVAL [262] checks each remembered candidate against the goal, rejecting a wrong
target and resuming exploration.
Updating also decides how long records are kept. Keeping every record makes memory large and slow to
search, while dropping records too early loses information that the robot may still need. MetaTool [235]
keeps a plan and tool outcomes in a scratchpad used only within the current episode. Other records compete
for a fixed amount of space. EEAgent [340] condenses reflections on successful and failed interactions
into a text memory of fixed size, and ActiveArena [186] compares rules for choosing what stays in such a
memory. A third kind of record persists beyond what the robot can currently see. SafeMem [180] keeps
objects that are out of view in its scene graph, revises their relations when changes are observed, and
requests replanning when the predicted risk is high. CoRelNav [100] retains shared evidence about object
instances. World Action Agent [440] retains reusable relational skills, procedures, and reference images,
which are checked against the current scene; scene-specific advice expires when the associated observation
or control state changes.
Memory Retrieval. Memory retrieval decides which stored records reach the next decision. As memory
grows, most of what it holds does not bear on the decision at hand, and passing the full history can bury
the few details that matter. Existing systems retrieve three kinds of content, evidence for the current
decision, the task state needed to continue work, and earlier results that can replace new computation.
The first kind is evidence for the current decision. Such evidence is usually selected by its relevance
to the current goal, location, or question. NavMCP [167] answers questions from a frozen navigation
model with keyframe-based summaries of the route so far, and keeps a list of unresolved goals across calls.
HFLEX-EQA [270] and NavProbe [210] select the graph or past-view evidence most relevant to each
navigation decision. EmbodiedLGR [282] searches a robot-built scene graph for relevant entries, and
CMMR-VLN [173] retrieves stored image and text experience for repeated navigation. HoloAgent-0 [464]
retrieves objects, rooms, views, and task history from a 3D spatial memory, and confirms them through
visual verification and active exploration. Across these systems, retrieval narrows a long history down to
the few records that bear on one decision.
The second kind is the task state needed to continue work. This kind matters when the executor cannot
keep track of the task itself, for example a skill that starts fresh at each call or an action model without
memory. BATON [391] retrieves this state at skill transitions. Instead of the whole episode, it retrieves
the setup and starting conditions recorded for the next skill, and checks them against a live wrist-camera
view. 2AM [112] retrieves it during a long task. It condenses its history into the target, the destination,
how many times a step has been repeated, and the current stage, and passes this summary as language
and optional 2D direction hints to an action model that has no memory of its own. In both systems, the
harness keeps the full history on its own side and passes the executor only the state that links the current
step to the rest of the task.
The third kind is an earlier result that can replace new computation. When a similar case has been handled
before, reusing the earlier result can be faster and cheaper than computing a new one, provided that
the result still applies. MeCo [332] retrieves the solution of a similar successful multi-robot task and
reuses its waypoint structure, falling back to the LLM from the failed step if validation rejects the transfer.
35


--- PAGE 36 ---
APEX [432] reuses the spatial evidence produced by earlier VLM updates, so that a drone can keep
exploring without waiting for each new update to finish. The two systems handle the question of whether
an earlier result still applies in different ways, MeCo by validating the reused solution and APEX by acting
on evidence that may be slightly older than the current scene. Related works are compared in Table 14.

7.3 Monitoring and Recovery
Monitoring judges whether execution is progressing, has finished, has failed, or has violated a constraint,
and recovery changes execution after a problem is found. We group existing methods into three categories
that follow this process from detection to repair. Execution Monitoring checks execution before, during, or
after an action. Response Selection turns a verdict into a response and checks that response before it runs.
Recovery Actions carry out the response, including where recovery starts and when to stop trying.
Execution Monitoring. Execution monitoring can check an action before it runs, while it runs, or after it
ends. The timing of a check determines which problems it can catch and what kind of report it produces.
Checks before execution act on proposed actions or routes that have not yet had any physical effect, and
usually accept, reject, or rank them. SafeHarness [390] tests proposed route against obstacles and returns
the rejected reasons so that the route can be revised. DV-VLN [194] answers true or false questions about
candidate navigation actions and reorders them accordingly. CoVer [157] prepares rephrasings of the
instruction in advance and uses a verifier to score candidate actions and instructions before each action
runs. Since these checks only need to choose among options, their outputs are simple decisions or scores.
Checks during execution give signals while the action is still under way. They mainly track constraint
36


--- PAGE 37 ---
violations and task progress, and some systems check at several levels at once. For constraints, DoReMi [94]
uses an LLM to write constraints for the next skill and a VLM to check them at regular intervals, stopping
the skill as soon as one is broken. Code-as-Monitor [458] writes visual constraints as code that can
detect a failure as it happens or before it happens. Meanwhile, Mission-Level Runtime Assurance [147]
checks constraints over mission events and reports ”unknown” when evidence is missing. For progress,
OnFly [455] checks the task stage in parallel with flight and reacts when it detects stop or lost conditions,
while Robometer [195] tracks progress with a score learned from expert labels and from comparisons
between successful and failed runs. ABot-AgentOS [321] combines several levels, checking at runtime, at
the skill level, and once more before allowing a task to finish. These checks mostly output signals to stop,
adjust, or report progress, since they must act before the motion ends.
Checks after execution can use the full record of what happened, which allows them to judge the outcome
and explain its cause. RoboLab [398], after first checking task assets and stability test results, judges the
outcome with generated task code that defines when a task has succeeded or should end. REFLECT [223]
summarizes an execution at the sensor, event, and subgoal levels to explain a failure to a correction planner.
AHA [64] gives failure-specific feedback in language based on sequences of key images. DQAF [245]
writes feedback after each episode for the next human operator. Overall, the timing of a check thus shapes
its output. Checks before execution choose among candidates, checks during execution signal whether to
stop or adjust, and checks after execution explain what happened.
Response Selection. Response selection decides what the system does after a problem is detected. It
covers three questions, how the detected problem reaches the agent, how the agent chooses a response,
and whether that response is checked before it runs. A detected problem can change behavior only if the
agent that chooses the next action can see it. Existing work either builds this link into the agent’s tools or
tests whether the link affects later decisions. Agentic AI for Robot Control [202] takes the first approach,
giving the agent tools to query robot status and events. Diagnosing Semantic Handoff Failures [285] takes
the second, checking whether a visual reachability check changes the agent’s next skill or route.
Once the agent sees the problem, it must choose a response. Existing systems either pick from a fixed set
of responses or let a planner revise the plan. Fixed responses are simple and predictable, while a planner
can produce responses that were not listed in advance. Goal2Skill [225] picks among retrying, adjusting,
and replanning, and A Closed-Loop Multi-Agent Framework for Robust Multi-Robot Manipulation [102]
separates local pose fixes from full replanning. Among planner-based systems, the planner is triggered by
an explanation of the failure in REFLECT [223], by a gap between the predicted and actual outcome in
WCM [51], and by a lack of progress in Stop Wandering [184].
A chosen response can also be wrong, so systems validate proposed actions and use execution evidence to
decide whether to retry. These checks rely on perception, sub-goal evidence, or the validity of tool inputs.
ETA [50] refuses to act when it cannot locate the target. ACE [165] uses tracked pick-and-place masks,
execution reports, and new observations to assess sub-goal completion and guide retries. HarnessVLN [44]
checks tool inputs and evidence before the robot moves or stops.
Recovery Actions. Once a failure is detected, the robot needs a recovery action that fixes it. Generally,
they differ in what they change, namely the input to the current policy, the executor, the task flow, or
something outside the policy. VLA-Corrector [302] changes the input, feeding stage-specific recovery
prompts to a frozen VLA. FLARE [445], VoLo [40], and ReCoVLA [106] change the executor, switching
to a trained reset adapter, a hand-designed skill guided by perception, or a simulation-trained correction
policy for each known failure type. AgentChord [395] changes the task flow, moving to recovery branches
compiled into its task graph without calling the LLM again. SERP [171] and Dream2Fix [170] change
something outside the policy, adjusting motion-cost parameters and applying small learned physical
corrections. In all cases, recovery is limited to the actions the system provides.
Recovery also needs to choose which state to resume from. A failed attempt often leaves the scene partly
changed, with objects moved or a step left half done. The robot can continue from this state and keep
the progress already made, or first restore the scene to a known state and start again. PhyAgentOS [221]
continues from the current state, diagnosing the failed attempt and resuming with new instructions.
37


--- PAGE 38 ---
HALTER [134] restores the scene first, using scene-graph histories to find what was completed, planning
reset skills, and checking that the reset worked. LIBERO-RECOVER [212] evaluates these two cases
separately, comparing recovery from a disturbed scene with an attempt that starts in a restored scene.
Finally, repeated attempts can fail again, so systems need a rule for when to stop. Existing systems stop
after a fixed budget, hand the task to a human, retry only under specific conditions, or aim for an end point
other than task completion. PEACE [328] lets the planner retry after a drone tool failure only within a
fixed budget. LabVLA [280] allows one validated retry during offline workflow-YAML generation before
returning the workflow for human review. A physical agentic grasping loop [353] retries only when the
gripper is empty, and otherwise stops or asks for clarification. SPINE [97] repairs robot software and
settings until the robot is ready for teleoperation, rather than until a task is completed. Related works are
compared in Table 15.

7.4 Skills
Orchestration, memory, and monitoring decide how a harness handles the current attempt; skills decide
what it brings to the next one. A skill is a pluggable package of procedural knowledge that the harness can
load or call. Loaded into the context, a textual skill shapes how the language model reasons about the task
and uses the harness’s tools; called as a function, an executable skill gives the model a reusable operation
38


--- PAGE 39 ---
on the robot. In the drawer example, the same experience can become a note to approach the handle from
below or a callable open_drawer program, and either can be swapped in without retraining the model.
Skills thus differ from memory, which records what happened, and from model weights, which a skill
leaves unchanged. A recent survey ranks code-as-policy systems by whether they use execution feedback,
persistent skill memory, and program search [129]; we focus instead on how skills enter, change within,
and leave a harness.
From Skills to Code as Policies. Early language-model harnesses treated skills as a fixed set of pretrained
primitives, and the model’s role was only to choose among them, as in SayCan [125]. Code generation
changed that role. In Code as Policies [196], the model composes perception and control APIs into a
program and defines missing functions on the fly, so the skill set becomes a vocabulary rather than a
menu. ProgPrompt [300] and VoxPoser [118] followed the same idea, expressing plans as programs
with assertions or as code that builds value maps for a motion planner. Because these programs are
written for one task and then discarded, later work let the program improve while the task is still running:
Act-Observe-Rewrite [155] rewrites a controller between trials from keyframes and logs, MEMENTO [314]
searches over a single code policy, and Agent as Policy [132] lets a coding agent write and revise programs
directly on a physical robot. Generated code thereby became a capable policy within a task, which raised
the question of what to keep across tasks.
Skill Libraries and Reuse. Reuse is the natural answer, and it requires organizing skills so that later
tasks can find them. Voyager [336] set the pattern in a Minecraft agent: a program joins a library once it
passes self-verification, and new tasks retrieve entries by embedding similarity. Skill libraries are now
common across other agent domains, where skills are distilled from agent experience and evolved alongside
reinforcement learning [380], or built from scientific resources [293] and human-created multimodal
material such as tutorials, repositories, and articles [66]. Robot systems adopted this library structure and
differ mainly in what they store and how an entry is admitted. RoboCoder [175] stores joint-level action
code accepted by a vision–language evaluator, while DROC [430] and ARCHITECT [34] store knowledge
distilled from human corrections, so that the same correction need not be given twice. Uni-Skill [385]
fills gaps in its library by implementing missing skills from a video-derived repository, and Teach and
Grow [249] admits a skill only after it passes a scoped outcome test on held-out states. A library can even
be built once and frozen, as CaP-X [74] does with nine helper functions distilled from successful rollouts,
or compressed into macros, as in SkillComposer [374]. Since entries in these libraries are checked on
admission and rarely revisited, a flawed skill persists until something revises it.
Skill Evolution. Skill evolution supplies that revision by feeding execution results back into the library.
Early systems revised skills with simple loops: LRLL [327] periodically abstracts accumulated experience
into new functions, and LYRA [240] relies on human corrections while re-testing earlier behavior.
Automating the reviser raised a new problem, because an agent that edits its own skills can also make
them worse, so recent systems differ chiefly in the gate a revision must pass. ASPIRE [228] lets
coding agents debug programs from per-primitive traces and promotes only repairs that validate and
generalize; RATs [435] promotes a skill after repeated success and deprecates it after repeated failure;
and RoboRSI [373] traces each failure to the earliest failing node of a task–skill hierarchy and keeps a
patch once it passes review, functional tests, and a replay of recent runs without regression. SkillOpt [402]
makes the analogy to training explicit, treating a skill document as the trainable state of a frozen agent and
accepting an edit only if it improves a held-out validation score, while Reconstruct, Practice, Go Real [358]
applies a similar rule across robot practice tasks and VASO [403] replaces the score with model-checker
counterexamples. The same loop extends from skills to the harness code around them, as in RHO [65],
which mutates whole policy repositories, and Zetta [60], which uses agents to diagnose failed rollouts and
evolve validated critic–recovery code that monitors execution and triggers local recovery while keeping
the base VLA frozen. Without such gates, updates need not accumulate: Explore, Execute, Evolve [388]
reports non-monotonic progress across successive updates, and even with replay, paired, and held-out
checks, a 123-round study found that accepted changes did not add up to success on its target task [339].
A useful skill library therefore depends as much on how revisions are tested and retired as on how they are
generated.
39


--- PAGE 40 ---
From Skill Evolution to Training. All of these mechanisms leave the model that writes skills unchanged,
which raises the question of whether skill experience could instead flow into weights. In practice, most
systems that do so train the executor rather than the coder. Agentic Skill Discovery [450] trains a
reinforcement-learning policy for each proposed skill, RoboClaw [178] and ENPIRE [383] fine-tune
robot policies from collected or agent-designed training runs, and HarnessPAI [356] and RACaP [192]
distill harness trajectories into a VLA or a smaller decision model. Training the coder itself is rarer.
RoboCodeX [242] and Uni-Skill [385] fine-tune code-generating models on curated or video-derived
programs, and CaP-X [74] applies reinforcement learning with environment rewards to a coding model on
three simulated tasks before transferring it to a physical arm. Training a multimodal coder continually on
its own rollouts, or folding an evolved skill library into its weights, would close the loop between skill
evolution and model learning. Related works are compared in Table 16.

7.5 Infrastructure
Beneath skills, every harness function rests on software infrastructure: the interface through which it
reaches robot capabilities, the perception and manipulation tools behind that interface, the services that
run learned policies, and the checks that stand between a proposed action and the hardware. In the drawer
example, open_drawer may expand into a segmentation call that finds the handle, a grasp generator that
proposes a pose, a motion planner that reaches it, and a VLA served on a remote GPU that performs the
pull. Each of these components can fail, add latency, or hide information from the agent. We discuss the
interface first, then the toolchains and policy services that dominate practical systems, and finally runtime
gating.
Capability Interfaces. The first layer of this infrastructure is the interface, which fixes what an agent can
express and what it learns back. EmbodiedTool [466] registers more than a hundred tools with capability
cards that state inputs, outputs, and trigger conditions; agents form valid calls almost always but choose
the right tool far less often. The return channel matters as much as the call: NavMCP [167] combines
structured intent, keyframe-grounded journey summaries, and persistent memory; replacing this complete
three-channel protocol with an episodic interface lowers question-answering accuracy from 74.0% to
59.1%. Granularity is a further choice. Show-Harness [46] exposes semantic Cartesian steps that an
interpreter projects into safe setpoints, and halving the step size raises zero-shot success from 60% to 82%.
The Model Context Protocol is becoming a common transport: RoboNeuron [90] derives typed MCP
tools from ROS 2 message schemas, and the ROS MCP Server [284] exposes topics, services, and actions
through rosbridge. On the robot side, Cardenas et al.’s ROSClaw [26] checks each call against velocity
bounds and an interface allowlist before dispatch, and EMOS [37] derives a capability profile from the
robot’s URDF and forward kinematics rather than from hand-written text.
41


--- PAGE 42 ---
Perception and Manipulation Toolchains. Behind the interface, code-as-policy harnesses act through a
stack of pretrained tools: open-vocabulary detection and segmentation such as Grounding DINO [216],
SAM 2 [276], and SAM 3 [27]; pointing models such as Molmo [58]; pose and depth estimation such as
FoundationPose [368] and FoundationStereo [369]; grasp generators such as AnyGrasp [67], Contact-
GraspNet [313], M2T2 [423], and GraspGen [243]; and GPU motion planners such as cuRobo [312].
CaP-X [74] exposes such a stack as stateless services behind a Python API, and its benchmark separates
tiers that receive privileged simulator masks and poses from tiers that must perceive from RGB-D. GaP [38]
organizes 51 perception, grasping, motion, and verification skills behind single gRPC methods with
shared types for poses, masks, point clouds, and trajectories, and translates them to ROS for physical
execution. Tools are also beginning to ship agent-facing endpoints: GraspGen provides a GPU server and
an MCP wrapper so that an LLM agent can request grasps without a local model. Physical Agency [75]
illustrates a complete physical deployment: detection, segmentation, stereo depth, grasp generation, and
task-and-motion planning run as servers on one workstation, while a real-time control computer drives the
arm.
These toolchains determine much of what a harness can achieve. Perception is the dominant source of
physical failure in several systems: most real-robot errors of VoxPoser [118] come from perception, point
tracking is the largest error source in ReKep [120], and OK-Robot [215] shows how errors compound when
off-the-shelf detection, segmentation, mapping, and grasping are chained without recovery. Privileged
state hides this cost. Kuroki and Tang [156] report 65.0% success with full simulator observations but
22.5% with RGB alone, and CaP-X trains its coding model on the privileged tier to avoid perception noise
before transferring to perceived inputs. An agent can repair a toolchain when the toolchain reports its
failures. ASPIRE [228] provides an execution engine with primitive-level traces, debugging, and repair
validation; adding the engine raises macro-average success on the evaluated LIBERO-Pro perturbations
from 14% to 62%. Physical Agency returns typed failures and overrides a reported grasp when the
gripper-width sensor shows it is empty. Across systems, coding agents repeatedly add the same hardening
around tools, including fallback chains from text-prompted to point-prompted segmentation, filters on
grasp orientation, inverse-kinematics checks before grasping, and sensor-checked grasps; RHO [65] and
ASPIRE move such logic into the tool wrappers themselves. Tool interfaces that report uncertainty and
failure, together with evaluations that separate privileged from perceived inputs, therefore make toolchain
results comparable across harnesses.
Policy Inference and Serving. Learned policies form the other half of this stack: when a harness calls
a VLA, the policy becomes a service with its own latency and capacity. The openpi remote-inference
interface [267] serves a checkpoint over a WebSocket that returns action chunks, keeping robot code free
of model dependencies, and LeRobot [23] separates a policy server from a robot client that requests a new
chunk when its action queue runs low. Asynchronous execution hides inference delay: SmolVLA [299]
uses this design to cut task time by about 30% at a small cost in success, real-time chunking [14, 15]
freezes actions committed during inference, and VLASH [315] conditions on the state those actions will
reach. Placement is a further design dimension. VLA-Perf [136] models latency across devices and
networks and finds that, for 𝜋0, an edge server over WiFi can outperform on-device inference; Gemini
Robotics [81] combines a cloud backbone with an on-robot decoder, and RoboECC [457] partitions a VLA
between edge and cloud according to predicted bandwidth. For fleets, ROSA [135] schedules VLA and
VLM components on a shared GPU pool under latency targets, Robion [1] partitions one GPU between
the vision–language and action stages to serve about twenty robots, and Armory [7] schedules batched
action-chunk inference across robots running at different rates. Runtime engineering narrows the remaining
gap: kernel-level optimization brings 𝜋0 to 27 ms on a consumer GPU [234], OxyGen [181] shares a
VLA’s cache between action and language heads, vla.cpp [248] runs quantized VLAs on embedded boards,
and BitVLA [337] matches a 4-bit baseline with ternary weights. These systems are mostly evaluated on
policy throughput; coupling them with agent harnesses will make serving latency part of the planning
budget.
Runtime Gating and Versioning. Once tools and policies are reachable, the harness must also decide
which proposed commands may reach the hardware, and this check is best placed outside the model.
42


--- PAGE 43 ---
RoboPAIR [283] motivates this placement by jailbreaking a self-driving language model, a GPT-4o-planned
ground robot, and a commercial quadruped in every attempted scenario. Safety Chip [405] compiles
expert-verified temporal-logic constraints into an automaton that prunes unsafe actions before execution,
and RoboGuard [277] model-checks plans against specifications derived by an isolated model, reducing
jailbreak success from 92.3% to 2.3%. SafeGate [255] fails closed by deferring to the user whenever a
hazard is not covered by a verified template, and RoboSafe [344] checks each action against generated
executable predicates. Other systems gate at the interface: ETA [50] separates what the agent wants to do
from what the interface permits, SafeHarness [390] rejects routes that violate obstacle clearance, and the
constrained-LLM design of Hong et al. [104] lets the model state only symbolic intent. CORE [278] instead
enforces inferred constraints in the controller through control barrier functions. The same gates also apply
when the harness itself changes. ETA requires a revised harness to pass contract checks, isolated review,
replay, and held-out evaluation, and in its reported runs no candidate passed; Harness Robotic OS [397]
proposes versioned, rollback-capable updates without demonstrating a revised executable. Related works
are compared in Table 17.
Table 17. Comparison of works on infrastructure (Section 7.5). Includes supporting tools and deployment systems,

7.6 Layered Systems
Layered systems organize the tools and policies above into layers that run at different rates. In the drawer
example, a planner decides to open the drawer, a VLA reaches for the handle, and an inverse-kinematics
solver tracks the pull, each at its own rate. We describe this stack as three layers: System 2 planners (VLMs,
LLMs, or generated code) that reason over tasks and subgoals, System 1 learned visuomotor policies,
typically VLAs, that react at control rate, and System 0 analytic controllers such as inverse kinematics,
motion planning, model predictive control, and whole-body control. Systems 1 and 2 follow the fast–slow
distinction of dual-process theories [141, 304]. System 0 adapts a label that Taniguchi et al. [317] use for
morphological computation; here it denotes explicit analytic control, and we avoid System 3 because it
already refers both to collective intelligence and to a runtime critic [413]. Existing VLA surveys contrast
monolithic with hierarchical models [291] but leave analytic control outside the comparison. The harness
is what connects the three layers, and this subsection examines what it passes between them, how often,
and which layer holds authority.
Layer Interfaces and Rates. The first design choice is what each layer passes downward and how often.
Helix [72] writes a single latent vector from a vision–language model at 7–9 Hz to a 200 Hz policy, and
GR00T N1 [254] passes intermediate embeddings from a 10 Hz reasoning module to an action module
producing 120 Hz chunks, which a whole-body inverse-kinematics controller tracks. Ground Slow, Move
Fast [367] makes all three layers explicit: a 2 Hz planner outputs a pixel goal, a 30 Hz policy produces
waypoints, and model predictive control tracks them. Other systems pass interpretable signals. Hi
Robot [297] sends language subtasks about once per second or when a user interjects, 𝜋0.5 [269] decodes
subtask text before actions within one model, NaVILA [52] sends parameterized language commands to a
locomotion policy, and HAMSTER [185] sends a few-point image path once per episode. In a controlled
comparison, VLA-OS [77] finds that low-level policies follow visual plans more faithfully than language
plans. Because the slow layer’s output is stale when used, RoboDual [21] and HiRT [433] train the fast
layer on delayed conditioning, and HarnessWAM [89] triggers deliberation on progress events, which
outperforms invoking the planner at fixed intervals. Latent interfaces carry more information but are
opaque to monitoring; text, paths, and waypoints can be checked at the cost of what they can express.
Agents Calling VLAs as Tools. Beyond integrated dual-system models, a layered system can also be as-
sembled from separate models, with the VLA exposed as one tool among others. Gemini Robotics 1.5 [320]
makes its action model available to an orchestrating reasoning model through language instructions, and
replacing a general model with the embodied-reasoning orchestrator halves the total failure rate. Harness
VLA [441] places a frozen VLA behind a vla_act primitive next to analytic motion primitives and verifies
each call; the VLA accounts for a minority of calls on its manipulation benchmarks. Physical Agency [75]
lets an agent choose between task-and-motion planning and a VLA, and overrides reported successes that
contradict grasp sensing. VLAs-as-Tools [166] returns progress signals that let a monitor advance or
replan with a few planner calls per episode. A systematic study of hierarchical VLA agents [108] finds
that termination rules and VLA steerability matter as much as planner size. Compared with an integrated
44


--- PAGE 45 ---
dual-system model, this arrangement keeps each layer replaceable and each call inspectable, but every
handoff must re-establish state and intent, as discussed under Orchestration.
Decision APIs. Between such orchestrators and their executors, a recent development adds a fast decision
layer. Decision models answer bounded questions over a supplied set of options instead of generating text.
TypeSafe’s Jev [326], described as a System One model, accepts a textual state and a batch of questions
that ask it to choose among options, place a case on an ordered scale, or judge whether a condition holds,
and returns a probability for every answer without producing output tokens. Liquid AI’s d1 [204] offers the
same three question types over text and images in a single forward pass, with text decisions in 200–300 ms,
and OpenAI has announced a comparable decision interface [259]. In a robot harness, such a model
sits beneath a slower planner: code proposes the legal candidates, the decision model selects one with a
probability, and the harness acts only above a confidence threshold. EmbodiedJev [69], an open-source
simulation workbench, applies this protocol to manipulation, letting Jev choose among skills, phases, or
Cartesian steps while inverse kinematics, safety checks, and success judgment remain in code. The idea
of restricting a language model to scored candidates predates these products. SayCan [125] combines
language-model likelihood with learned affordances over a fixed skill library, MOKA [207] asks a VLM
to choose among marked keypoints, and KnowNo [279] and IntroPlan [198] turn option likelihoods into
conformal prediction sets that trigger requests for help. Dedicated decision models make this pattern
cheap enough to run at every step. Their thresholds are meaningful once calibration is measured on the
robot’s own decisions, since a candidate’s probability expresses preference among the offered options
rather than the chance of physical success.
Code as the Upper Layer and Outlook. At the top of the stack, code can itself serve as System 2. The code-
as-policies line discussed under Skills composes System 0 primitives in generated programs; ReKep [120]
sharpens the division of labor by having a VLM write keypoint-constraint functions once per task with its
solver running at about 10 Hz and keypoint tracking and position control at 20 Hz; Instruct2Act [117]
composes perception models and motion primitives in generated code. AgentChord [395] compiles
monitored task graphs with precompiled recovery branches and constrained solvers, so that detected
violations are handled without a new language-model call. Programs that call VLAs alongside analytic
primitives, as in Harness VLA, combine both: code remains inspectable and verifiable, while the VLA
covers contact-rich segments that are hard to specify. A natural direction is a stack in which VLAs serve
as callable lower layers, programs provide the upper layer, and an agent revises both offline through skill
evolution. Each choice across the chapter carries a cost in this stack. A bridge between executors broadens
successor states but consumes motion time; retrieved history preserves a commitment but can propagate
stale evidence; editable code can repair a recurring failure but can also change the monitor that judges
it. The strongest comparisons isolate the changed layer or interface, hold the others fixed, and test later
behavior beyond the feedback used for optimization. Related works are compared in Table 18.
Table 18. Comparison of works on layered systems (Section 7.6). Update timing identifies the refreshed module;
rates refer to the reported configurations. Architecture studies list alternatives. NR: not reported.
Work
Link
Upper layer
Lower layer
Passed down

8 Outlook: What Comes Next for Agentic Robots?
Agentic robots connect general-purpose reasoning models with robot policies, tools, and physical execution.
However, integrating these components does not necessarily make a robotic system more capable. It
remains difficult to determine what the agent contributes, whether its decisions can be executed reliably on
physical robots, and how well the system performs under limited resources or on unfamiliar platforms.
These challenges motivate four research directions.
8.1 Fair Benchmarking
What does a task success rate actually tell us about an agentic robot? Consider two systems evaluated on
the same manipulation benchmark. One may rely on a stronger VLA executor, while the other uses an agent
to coordinate multiple weaker skills, gather additional observations, and recover from failures. Similar
scores can reflect very different capabilities. CaP-X [74] evaluates agents that generate robot-control
programs, while Harness VLA [441] and RoboHarness [116] study how agents coordinate robotic policies
and skills. Their results depend not only on the agent but also on the underlying executors, available
tools, observation access, and allowed retries. Task success alone therefore cannot show which part of the
system made the difference.
To compare agents fairly, we need to examine their decisions under comparable conditions. Using the same
executors, observation access, and interaction budgets makes it easier to assess planning, tool selection,
and recovery. Comparing a system with and without memory or replanning can further show what each
component contributes. However, controlled comparisons alone are not enough, since choosing and
combining different models and tools is itself an important capability. End-to-end evaluations should
therefore report the resources available to each system, including additional observations, computational
costs, execution time, and human assistance. Such comparisons can reveal whether the benefits of
additional reasoning and tool use justify their costs.
Even with careful comparisons, a benchmark may tell us little about the agent if its tasks can be completed
by a single fixed policy. Some robot benchmarks focus on task completion without requiring the agent to
handle missing information, unsuitable executors, or failed actions. More informative benchmarks could
46


--- PAGE 47 ---
introduce unexpected environmental changes, incomplete observations, and tasks that require coordination
across different skills. For agents that collect data, update models, or develop new skills, evaluation should
also examine whether these changes improve performance on unseen tasks. This would help establish
when an agent is useful and whether its improvements generalize beyond the original setting.
8.2 Real-robot Agentic Robotics
A plan that makes sense to an agent may not always be executable by a robot. A reasoning model might
request a grasp beyond the robot’s reach or select a skill based on an outdated observation. Agentic
robots may combine reasoning models, VLAs, world-action models, and specialized robot skills, each
with different capabilities and execution requirements. ReKep [120] uses model-generated constraints
to guide robot motion, while Harness VLA [441] allows an agent to call a VLA alongside other robot
operations. Yet choosing a suitable executor is only part of the problem. A valid command may still fail
during physical execution, and the scene may change before the agent receives feedback.
Robot interfaces therefore need to describe more than the names and inputs of available skills. An agent
should know when a skill can be used, what conditions it requires, and how to tell whether execution has
finished. For example, a failed grasp may require another observation, a revised grasp command, or a
different skill. The right response depends on what actually happened: the object may have moved, or
the previous command may still be running. Sending a stop request does not guarantee that the robot
has stopped moving. Before replanning, the agent needs feedback that confirms the current state and the
effects of actions already taken.
These difficulties become harder to address when moving from simulation to physical robots. Simulators
can provide accurate object positions and clearly defined success conditions, while real robots must
often estimate these from noisy camera images and sensor readings. Small perception errors, calibration
differences, and unexpected contacts can cause an otherwise reasonable plan to fail. Future systems should
recognize when their observations are insufficient, check important assumptions before execution, and
use physical feedback to correct their decisions. Real-robot evaluations should also make clear what
information the agent receives, what the lower-level controllers handle, and whether the agent can recover
when its assumptions turn out to be wrong.
8.3 Efficient Robot Use
A robot may complete a motion in a few seconds, yet spend much longer waiting for the agent to decide
what to do next. Repeated model calls, visual observations, and tool interactions can introduce significant
delays, especially when a task requires many small actions. Recent work has begun addressing this
problem by reducing unnecessary high-level reasoning. HarnessWAM [89] uses a lightweight progress
estimator to monitor execution and invokes its VLM task manager when important events occur. Similarly,
VLAs-as-Tools [166] provides execution feedback that allows an agent to replan when needed, rather than
continuously checking the robot’s progress. Efficiency therefore depends not only on how fast a model
runs, but also on when it is called.
The main challenge is deciding when another model call is worth the delay. A robot following a familiar
manipulation sequence may continue with lightweight monitoring, while an unexpected obstacle or failed
action may require a new decision from the reasoning model. Calling the model too often wastes time, but
waiting too long can leave the robot following a plan that no longer works. The timing should depend on
task progress, uncertainty, and the possible consequences of a delayed response.
Efficiency also depends on where computation takes place. Large models running in the cloud may
offer stronger reasoning but introduce communication delays, while smaller local models can respond
more quickly with limited computational resources. Future systems could combine these options, using
local components for time-sensitive decisions and stronger models when additional reasoning is needed.
Evaluations should therefore consider the full execution process, including reasoning time, tool calls,
communication overhead, and physical task completion time. Fewer model calls or faster inference do not
necessarily improve efficiency if they lead to more failures, retries, or longer task execution.
47


--- PAGE 48 ---
8.4 Cross-Embodiment Generalization
An agent that works well with one robot may struggle when given a different platform. Even a simple
task, such as reaching a target location, requires different actions from a wheeled robot, a humanoid,
or a drone. The high-level goal may remain similar, but the available sensors, control interfaces, and
physical constraints are different. RobotWorld [404] evaluates general-purpose agents on 84 simulated
tasks spanning manipulation, mobile manipulation, locomotion, driving, and aerial control. What remains
unclear is whether the same agent can adapt to a new robot without major changes to its planning and
tool-use setup.
One promising direction is to enable agents to understand the capabilities of unfamiliar robots before using
them. An agent could identify the robot’s available sensors, movement capabilities, and supported skills,
then plan around those capabilities. For example, a wheeled robot may need to navigate around obstacles
to inspect an area, while a drone could approach it from above. The goal is similar, but the actions and
physical constraints are different. The agent must therefore adjust its plan rather than simply translate the
same commands into another robot’s control interface.
Another important question is how much of an agent’s previous experience can be reused. High-level
strategies, such as gathering additional observations when information is missing or checking why an
action failed, may remain useful across different robots. In contrast, specific motion commands and
control parameters often depend on the robot’s embodiment. Future systems should retain general task
knowledge without assuming that the same execution details apply to every platform. Evaluations could
test agents on previously unseen robots, measuring task success, the amount of additional configuration or
training required, and whether experience from other robots improves performance.
9 Conclusion
This paper examines how agents support robot execution and learning. We trace the evolution from
robot policies to agentic systems and clarify the scope of agentic robots. We organize existing work into
seventeen overlapping categories across four functional domains: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness.
These categories cover model learning and decisions, data collection and preparation, the construction of
worlds, tasks, and tests, and the software that coordinates robot capabilities.
Across these approaches, an agent’s decisions depend on what it can observe, what the robot can do, and
what feedback it receives. During execution, agents coordinate tools and skills, check progress, and use
feedback to decide whether to continue, gather more information, or recover from failure. Beyond the
current task, stored experience can guide later decisions and changes to data, training procedures, skills,
models, and software. These changes need to be tested on new tasks and conditions, while checking that
previously learned capabilities are preserved.
Building on this analysis, we discuss four directions for future research: fair benchmarking, real-robot
deployment, efficient robot use, and cross-embodiment generalization. Future work needs to establish
when agents improve performance under comparable conditions and how they handle uncertainty and
failures on physical robots. It should also examine whether these improvements justify the time and
resources used, and how well agents adapt to unfamiliar robots. We hope this work helps researchers
connect these questions and develop more capable agentic robots. Progress in agentic robotics could bring
us closer to intelligent systems that turn knowledge into action, learn from experience, and adapt to the
changing physical world.
